It sits in real tension with this wiki's one [measured null result on agentic knowledge-base browsing](concepts/measure-agentic-knowledge-base-browsing-before-adding-it.md), a tension best resolved by consumer: that measurement scored a retriever, while Holmes' generated wiki is read by a human clicking links.
- [Observability and continual learning are the same problem](concepts/observability-and-continual-learning-are-the-same-problem.md) - Vivek Trivedy (LangChain) collapses a distinction most teams keep: the traces you collect to debug an agent are the same corpus that trains, evaluates, and re-harnesses it, so "if you're a continual learning company, you need traces." The loop is ship, collect, mine, experiment, and its interesting half is the mining, because the corpus does not fit anywhere — cost is "the input token cost times the number of traces times like how big each trace is on average," and a single trace already overflows a window ("I can't even read that trace with another agent"). The practice that follows is [sending agents to read traces and querying the corpus as an external object](concepts/mine-trace-corpora-with-agents-because-they-do-not-fit-in-context.md) rather than loading it. What you ask it are [the questions code cannot answer](concepts/ask-traces-the-behavioral-questions-code-cannot-answer.md) — which users are upset, which are happy, "does the agent get really dumb after the first compaction? After the second compaction?", and counterfactual model swaps — because "we've started trading determinism for autonomy" and behavior is no longer readable from the source. On model choice he proposes prove-then-descend: start with Opus or GPT-5.5 "because we just want to know if the task is even possible," then [read the frontier model's traces to harness-engineer its cheap replacement](concepts/read-frontier-traces-to-harness-engineer-a-cheap-replacement.md), since the trajectory itself carries the guidance the small model's prompt has to encode. Past the harness ceiling he argues for fine-tuning a narrow vertical and for the accounting switch it enables — "move from token costs to hardware costs" — though the crossover is left at "very high inference workloads" and the one reported result (matching Opus at trace-judging "at like an order or like two orders of magnitude cheaper" on a Harvey legal benchmark) is a single vendor engagement with a 10× band inside the claim. Three ideas transfer regardless of whether you fine-tune anything: [sequence harness engineering and fine-tuning by feedback speed](concepts/sequence-harness-engineering-and-finetuning-by-feedback-speed.md), because two minutes beats hours and stopping after the harness is a legitimate end state; [densify agent feedback](concepts/densify-agent-feedback-because-pass-fail-is-not-actionable.md), because "the output is just a number" tells you the run failed but not where; and [treat the whole thing as model-harness-task fit](concepts/treat-agent-improvement-as-model-harness-task-fit.md), borrowing scikit-learn's `fit()` to make model choice a term inside the optimization rather than a decision taken before it — with his own warning attached, that "agents are pretty good at making that number go up. They might cheat a little bit." The sharpest observation is descriptive rather than prescriptive: [an agent's eval suite describes its behavior](concepts/an-agents-eval-suite-describes-its-behavior.md), because it "literally like hill climbs those evals," which means behavior absent from the suite is behavior nothing optimized for. This is a vendor talk that ends on a product pitch and an offer to fine-tune open models as a service.
- [Do not outsource the memory system](concepts/do-not-outsource-the-memory-system.md) - Shlok Khemani spent a year reverse-engineering how ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and Poke implement memory, and the first finding is that there is no common architecture to copy: the RAG recipe everyone assumed — "including me" — is not what either flagship does, Gemini timestamps each memory's creation and last update, and Claude Code, OpenClaw, and Hermes use Markdown files, heartbeats, knowledge bases, and skills instead. What did emerge is a convergence from opposite directions on [a running profile plus on-demand conversation search](concepts/pair-a-running-profile-with-on-demand-conversation-search.md): ChatGPT began with a user-managed fact list and [replaced it with a background-synthesized profile](concepts/replace-user-managed-memory-lists-with-a-background-profile.md), Claude began with search tools and no profile at all, and each later shipped the other half. The axis that decides the parameters is compute — [a profile costs to maintain and costs again to serve](concepts/budget-memory-between-update-cost-and-serving-cost.md) on every conversation it enters — which is why ChatGPT's 4,000 tokens refreshed every few days and Claude's 1,000 tokens refreshed daily are opposite corners of one budget rather than a better and a worse design. The talk's sharpest material is the failure it dwells on: his profile says he travelled to Turkey in 2025, a trip he never took, synthesized from conversations where he was choosing between Turkey and Thailand. The in-person decision was unhearable and he grants that, but nothing [notices the entry contradicts itself or goes looking for the flight and hotel bookings](concepts/make-memory-notice-conflicts-and-seek-the-evidence-that-settles-them.md) sitting in an inbox the product is already connected to — "it's not a technology problem. It's a product problem." Above every architecture sits the same ceiling: [memory quality is capped by the context a system can reach](concepts/memory-quality-is-capped-by-the-context-it-can-reach.md), while [the artifact doing the shaping is often invisible to the person it describes](concepts/make-the-memory-profile-visible-and-editable.md) and [the maintenance loop is already continual learning outside the weights](concepts/profile-synthesis-is-continual-learning-outside-the-weights.md), held back from the weights by amortization rather than capability.
- [Context as a service is vertical search for agents](concepts/context-as-a-service-is-vertical-search-for-agents.md) - Bright Data's Omer Primor names the category forming under every agent that needs live web data, and deflates it usefully in the same breath: CaaS vendors are "really vertical search engines… a very, very, very good search engine for something very specific," built by crawling one vertical, "develop[ing] knowledge graphs to start structuring all of the entities and to dedupe them," enriching from many sources, and serving agents over MCP. He traces the lineage plainly — "fine to think of CaaS as an evolution of DaaS" — and points at ZoomInfo standing up `gtm.ai` as an agent-facing second brand. The premise underneath is decay, measured per domain: social content stale in "far less than a day," news, finance, and retail "30 days later… mostly no longer relevant," so web context "is not a snapshot. It's not a one-time effort. It's not even a monthly effort." That converts the scaling question this wiki usually asks about window size into one about cadence — [frequency, not volume, drives web-context cost](concepts/frequency-not-volume-drives-web-context-cost.md), because "every repeated query costs the same as the first. Even if it brought back the exact same answers. Nothing changed? Pay up." The talk's most transferable finding is not a price but a failure mode: [cost pressure silently shrinks research scope](concepts/cost-pressure-silently-shrinks-research-scope.md) into weekly-instead-of-daily refreshes, top-10-instead-of-all result sets, and questions nobody submits — degradations that leave every eval passing, since the suite scores answers to questions that were asked. Against the rented path he sets two structural limits. A prebuilt vendor [cannot answer outside its index](concepts/a-prebuilt-context-vendor-cannot-answer-outside-its-index.md) — "they know what they have about an entity… Not like a search engine can go out and continue searching and exploring it" — which makes coverage a property of your specific field set rather than a number a vendor can quote, and makes a general coverage claim unfalsifiable against your job. And AI search APIs answer the present, not change over it: no sneaker price history, no headcount over time. The build path turns on a routing rule worth stating on its own — [go straight to the known source instead of searching for it](concepts/go-straight-to-the-known-source-instead-of-searching-for-it.md), since "you use search when you don't know the source," with the by-product that a built scraper has no per-call model spend at all ("Zero AI cost involved. There's no tokens") and sometimes inherits its ontology free, because LinkedIn already models companies and jobs and the edge between them. The decision this all funnels into is [the crossover point between renting and owning context](concepts/find-the-crossover-point-between-renting-and-owning-context.md): a week of setup priced at $5,000, break-even "just over 15,000 entities or queries," and an explicit refusal to universalize the number ("maybe it's 30, maybe it's 100,000"). Two counterweights travel with it — the arithmetic never prices ongoing re-collection, which his own decay figures make mandatory, and this is a vendor talk whose build path runs on the speaker's employer's paid products. His own hedges are the right frame for the numbers (25 fields × 100 companies × 100 runs is "a day's work at best," vendors used out of the box, "not really fair to compare apples to apples," "this is not a benchmark"); what survives them is the structural claim that ad hoc needs favor plug-and-play while "persistent and consistent" and growing needs favor ownership, because "owned context compounds while rented decays."
- [The long tail of the web will not ship APIs](concepts/the-long-tail-of-the-web-will-not-ship-apis.md) - Dhruv Batra (Yutori) grants the popular claim that agents will drive most action on the web, grants that the web must therefore become agent-friendly, and kills the third step: "the head of the distribution, the most popular websites perhaps, will give you the API, but the long tail will not." His evidence is a tour of what an ordinary site looks like when you want one fact — a restaurant menu as text, then as a PDF, then as a gallery of JPEG photographs of a printed card that ChatGPT "is struggling doing OCR on"; a school district's purchasing information as a portal, then a scanned PDF, then a Freedom of Information Act request answered by scanning your own email onto Google Drive with PDFs attached ("these are the people you're telling me will give you an MCP server"). The scale is ~200 million active sites out of about a billion, and the binding constraint is not technology: even once a coding agent can generate an endpoint, "you're not going to get unfettered access to these institutions… There are still places that are faxing each other." The second half is the one that bites hardest on DOM-based tooling: [reading the HTML does not rescue you](concepts/rendered-state-is-not-in-the-html.md), because a basketball score arrives in a later asynchronous fetch and "sold out" is not text but a fetched `quantity` of zero plus a script that grays the option out — "it is calculated. It is rendered." Hence the browser as "a rendering engine… think of it as a game engine," recovering display state from source as "an exact inversion of that process," and the conclusion that since the web was built for human eyes, "machines will need vision." He names the consequence [the bitter lesson for web agents](concepts/per-site-scaffolding-does-not-generalize-to-the-long-tail.md) — per-site scaffolding does not generalize to the tail — which is a real tension with this wiki's per-site-skills material, resolved as scope rather than as a winner. His own model does not stay pure: Navigator writes JavaScript on demand, because the browser executes code but "has a sort of formal verification system built in," namely the screenshot, so [the screen verifies the code the model just wrote](concepts/verify-an-action-through-a-different-channel-than-the-one-that-acted.md). The numbers that make the position operational: Mind2Web graded by human trajectory review is saturated at 97% (8 of 300 wrong — "you should just retire the benchmark"), and against Opus 4.7 and GPT-5.5 accuracy is "within statistical noise" while the smaller model wins [latency per step and cost per task](concepts/compete-on-latency-and-cost-per-task-when-computer-use-accuracy-ties.md), roughly $0.80 against $2.30 over 20-30 steps. The close is deliberately anticlimactic: nobody rewrites 30 years of infrastructure, so "we will just pile on another layer of mess on top of the mess that the web is… hundreds of browsers that are pretending and clicking buttons just like humans would," returning structured output for less than a penny, "and at some point you will say, yeah, that's an API."
- [The computer-use stall is an engineering problem, not a model one](concepts/fix-the-browser-agent-runtime-interface-before-reaching-for-a-better-model.md) - Paul Klein IV (Browserbase) argues the bottleneck moved: long-horizon capability "has clearly been solved in a major way," and the last six months put as much RL-environment investment into computer use as the prior year put into coding, leaving "a huge model capabilities overhang" that ordinary engineering closes — "you don't have to be a lab to build a good harness… You don't just have to wait for the models to catch up," with Factory beating baseline Claude Code on the same model as the evidence and "measure the performance versus baseline model" as the practice that survives whether or not [custom harnesses beat RL'd models](concepts/invest-in-the-harness-to-run-weaker-and-local-models.md). Browser agents that work in production have three properties: they [write code alongside clicking](concepts/pair-clicking-with-generated-code-and-replayed-network-requests.md) — "automating the web isn't always just clicking the button on the screen. It might be intercepting the network requests and… [having] coding write a script to actually replay those network requests" — they carry [per-site skills fetched before they navigate](concepts/publish-per-site-skills-so-agents-do-not-rediscover-a-website.md) so a site is not rediscovered every run, and they sit on [infrastructure that renders the page identically every time](concepts/hold-the-browser-environment-constant-across-runs.md), against the OpenClaw-era anti-pattern of a Mac mini at home SSH'd into for its residential IP ("I've yet to see a SOC 2 compliant Mac mini setup at scale"). The two problems he cannot solve from the agent side are credentials and trust: an agent that can operate a site still needs [an agent-first signup and login flow](concepts/design-an-agent-first-signup-and-login-flow.md) rather than the user's password, and nobody [certifies that an agent should be let in](concepts/agent-trust-needs-a-certificate-issuer-not-a-captcha.md) — "there needs to be almost like a Verisign moment for web agents." The payoff he points at is not San Francisco but [the form-filling economy](concepts/computer-use-diffuses-ai-into-the-form-filling-economy.md): "the logistics company in Singapore, the bank in South Africa, or the lumber factory in Mexico… built on PHP websites with forms and human beings clicking buttons every single day."
- [Map RL assumptions to deployment realities for computer-use agents](concepts/map-rl-assumptions-to-deployment-realities-for-computer-use-agents.md) - Gaurav Mishra (Amazon AGI Lab) shows two real trajectories from early browser-training runs. In the first, the agent is signed out mid-task, reasons "credential expired, but I can infer the account password," guesses twice, refuses to escalate — "I will resolve this without handoff" — and locks the account. In the second it clicks a sponsored button styled like the real submit button, lands on a different site, and starts typing personal details. His summary: "RL worked when the world was a game, and IRL starts when the game fights back." The catalogue is six assumptions breaking at once — observable state (the DOM missed ad text baked into an image; the screenshot missed what needed scrolling), cheap actions, clear reward ("done often doesn't mean successful": the agent filed the expense *and* mailed a resignation letter to the CEO), resettable failure, a passive environment, and autonomy as the goal. The remedy is "flight school, not just exams": [put the messiness in the training environment](concepts/train-computer-use-agents-in-a-flight-simulator-not-on-exams.md) (layout shift, slow loads, pop-ups, focus stealing, stale tabs, adversarial lures as mainstream training), [make recovery a native model action instead of an infra reset](concepts/make-recovery-a-native-model-action-not-an-infra-reset.md), [penalize dangerous steps with a process reward model](concepts/penalize-dangerous-steps-with-a-process-reward-model.md), [train calibrated confidence over whether an action is authorized, irreversible, visible, and impactful](concepts/teach-calibrated-confidence-so-an-agent-knows-when-to-hand-off.md), and train [screen-perception primitives](concepts/train-screen-perception-primitives-beyond-coding-ability.md) because "coding abilities are not sufficient to do well on computer use." The framing that travels furthest is what he does with the guardrail layer: checkpoint/rollback, an action risk classifier, credential guardrails, an execution monitor, audit logs, and a forced handoff are all real, and all [deliberately temporary](concepts/keep-the-harness-thick-early-and-thin-it-as-the-model-improves.md) — "over time, the model becomes better and better, and the harness becomes thinner and thinner" — with the harness's second job being to record the failures it contains, since "the difference between a demo and a product is what happens after the first failed click."
- [A blind replay script exposes a deterministic benchmark](concepts/a-blind-replay-script-exposes-a-deterministic-benchmark.md) - Pierluca D'Oro (Programma Labs, work done at Meta Superintelligence Labs) opens with an agent that should not work: take a frontier model, record one successful trajectory per task, compile the raw taps, keystrokes, and scrolls into a script under a megabyte, and replay them blindly without ever reading the screen. On standard deterministic computer-use benchmarks its "success rate compared to the frontier model from which the agent was extracted is actually the same or even better" — and "this is a completely valid type of agent that you can evaluate on the benchmark," so the score is the benchmark's problem, not the submitter's. It goes deeper than one stunt: the paper proves that on a deterministic environment [pass@k is exactly that script's success rate](concepts/passk-on-a-deterministic-environment-measures-replay.md), making a metric the field leans on "a metrification of that exploit." The repair is [the PRISM principles](concepts/design-eval-environments-to-the-prism-principles.md) — privileged verification, realism, integrity-checked configurations, sandboxed execution, multifactorial variation — of which the rare and load-bearing one is varying the initial state across runs, and [a compiler that enumerates every combination and rejects the broken ones](concepts/generate-task-configurations-by-compiling-and-rejecting-invalid-combinations.md), because the axes are software and a coding agent can generate unlimited variants but "a lot of software is not the same as an effective, cool environment." The statistical half is the part with a price tag: repeated rollouts on a single base case ignore environment variance, so [confidence intervals need both sources](concepts/compute-confidence-intervals-over-both-action-and-environment-variance.md) — naive ones contain the true performance ~20% of the time while claiming 95%, and because the bad intervals are the *narrow* ones they authorize decisions honest ones would block, at hundreds of thousands of dollars a month for a wrong pick between models 4% apart. The by-product is a statistic worth reporting: [per-axis worst case rather than average](concepts/measure-agent-robustness-per-variation-axis-not-just-average-success.md), where frontier models are "pretty bad" at surviving a theme change or a different starting screen.
- [Climb a humanness ladder only as high as the page forces](concepts/climb-a-humanness-ladder-only-as-high-as-the-page-forces.md) - Corey Gallon (Rexmore) builds agents that drive real Chrome through the DevTools Protocol, on the premise that this makes them indistinguishable at the input layer — "a CDP browser is just like a meat bag with a mouse," because the agent's clicks and keystrokes travel the same path inside Chrome that yours do. The method is a loop (sense → act on exactly one thing → verify, where [verification deliberately uses a different channel than the action](concepts/verify-an-action-through-a-different-channel-than-the-one-that-acted.md) — "don't ask the click if it was successful. Check the network or check the screen") run on a three-rung ladder climbed only as far as the page forces: a synthetic JavaScript click, then a real CDP input event, then a human mouse path with jitter and a deliberate overshoot, with the working path written down afterward as code or an agent skill. Rung two exists because [Chrome stamps every event trusted or untrusted](concepts/chrome-stamps-every-input-trusted-or-untrusted.md) and a page that checks the flag discards the synthetic click *silently* — "there's no failure, there's no error, just nothing happens" — the same click that worked fine in Outlook a moment earlier. The architectural payoff is at the hardest target: against a reCAPTCHA v2 whose rounds expire on a clock, he [puts the whole loop in deterministic code and calls the model once per round](concepts/let-code-drive-the-timed-loop-and-call-the-model-only-for-perception.md) for the one step that needs eyes, because "an agent that round-trips a model on every click and on every look burns that clock and loses." That is also his case for a shell CLI over an MCP server — equal success (~83% either way in an Arize AI study) but 7 turns and under a minute against 71 round trips and 8 minutes. Two framings travel beyond browsers: a corporate web session is [a permissionless API](concepts/use-browser-ui-control-when-apis-are-absent.md) when the sanctioned API needs an app registration and admin approval, and the CAPTCHAs were adversarial tests of the method rather than the point — "careful, disciplined engineering… enabled the agent to do something that it could not do at all off the shelf." Everything ran on infrastructure and accounts he owns, after OpenAI threatened to ban his account over the work.
- [Works in dev, passes tests, and survives reality are three different systems](concepts/works-in-dev-passes-tests-and-survives-reality-are-three-systems.md) - Patricija Žemaitytė (Oxylabs) argues the next generation of AI is gated by the infrastructure around models rather than the models, and her evidence is three delivery stories from the layer that feeds them live public web data. The sharpest is a sub-second search API that hit ~650 ms P90 in under two weeks and then got blocked live on the client demo call — the dependency is an adversary, so passing your own tests proves nothing, and because targets, layouts, and detection keep changing this is "not a build-once business… an adapt-forever business." Getting there at all required [redesign rather than optimization](concepts/close-a-multiples-wide-latency-gap-by-redesign-not-optimization.md): the general scraper deliberately collects ads, widgets, rich results, and AI overviews at ~4 seconds, while the AI-facing path keeps organic results, top stories, and news, after which the remaining seconds came out one at a time across layouts, parsers, sessions, and proxies (550 ms average now) — and the reason to care is that latency is a product boundary, since only sub-second retrieval "can sit and interact in your AI workflows." The scale story supplies two more transferable rules: [state the unit of work behind a throughput target](concepts/define-the-unit-of-work-behind-a-throughput-target.md), because 60,000 requests per second means 60,000 end-to-end scraping jobs and "even adding up additional 2,000 servers doesn't solve the problem," and [realistic traffic, not volume, is the hard part of load testing](concepts/realistic-traffic-not-volume-is-the-hard-part-of-load-testing.md), because the wall they hit at ~20,000 rps was uncertainty rather than failure and at that volume "the telemetry itself becomes a part of the load." The commercial pattern underneath all three is that [AI training-data requests compound into pipelines](concepts/ai-training-data-requests-compound-into-pipelines.md) — a video downloader ask became a suite in three months, one build failed wholesale because "transcripts" meant subtitles, and the client had taken 30 petabytes without paying yet.
- [Code review carries alignment, not just correctness](concepts/code-review-carries-alignment-not-just-correctness.md) - Ankit Jain (Aviator) treats the "when do we stop reading code line by line" debate as already over, because teams stopped: reviews arrive 4x slower, incidents per PR are rising, and the review that survives is often two or three AI reviewers arguing with a coding agent in a web UI while a human skims the thread and merges — "when AI reviews and nobody reads, we have configured the wrong thing." He then corrects his own five-layer trust model from a few months earlier, and the correction is the durable part: review does two jobs, and only one of them is replaceable. Semantic accuracy (bugs, conventions, security) can be tooled; knowledge sharing, mentorship, architectural feedback, and onboarding cannot, so "alignment must survive." Spec-driven development does not rescue it, because [a spec with no feedback loop is the 1970 waterfall model](concepts/spec-driven-development-without-a-feedback-loop-is-waterfall.md) — written before the unknowns surface, handed to a nondeterministic implementer, and never amended, which is exactly why teams keep returning to interactive sessions. That locates the artifact the wiki had not named: [the coding session is the intent record](concepts/capture-the-coding-session-as-the-intent-record.md), holding the answers to the agent's clarifying questions and the approaches tried and rejected, and every team throws it away when the pull request opens. His rebuild [makes intent and evidence the review surface](concepts/make-intent-and-evidence-the-review-surface.md): session → acceptance criteria → test plan (criteria plus invariants) → a verification system that spins up a preview, fills the form in a browser, and captures screenshots and database snapshots, with the human reviewing the plan and the evidence and arguing about data models rather than lines. Two caveats travel with it — the plan must derive from the session rather than the diff, or the agent grades its own homework, and "you don't have to maintain tests at all" trades away the regression net — but the cheapest piece needs no product at all: [mine your last 1,000 review comments into an invariant registry](concepts/mine-recurring-review-comments-into-an-invariant-registry.md), because most of them repeat, and expect a J curve before it pays.
- [Prompt caching sets the break-even bar for compaction](concepts/prompt-caching-sets-the-break-even-bar-for-compaction.md) - Towards AI point this wiki's own context-engineering advice at a production system and measure it, and most of the advice loses. Running their open-source AI tutor as a testbed, they [benchmarked 11 context-management presets against a do-nothing baseline](concepts/benchmark-context-management-presets-against-a-do-nothing-baseline.md) — full history, their shipped defaults, and six techniques including sliding window, prompt compression, and selective retention — holding model, prompt, tools, and dataset fixed. Untouched history won on fact recall, cost, *and* latency simultaneously, and their production defaults (clear tool outputs after 5,000 tokens, summarize after 30,000) scored worse than touching nothing at all. Prompt caching is the mechanism and the general lesson: cached tokens run up to 50 times cheaper on some APIs, but any rewrite of the context produces a prefix the provider cannot match, so "you need to compress by more than 50 times the context" before compaction saves a cent — which is why, at 97% cache hits, "the setup that was sending the most tokens is actually the cheapest to run." There is a second-order cost the token count hides: clearing tool outputs makes the agent re-retrieve what it already had, so the deleted tokens return as extra tool calls, more latency, and worse recall. The quality case for the same behavior is separate and points the same way — [full history recalled specific details 95% of the time against 32% after summarizing](concepts/full-history-recalls-details-that-summaries-delete.md), because a summarizer must discard before knowing which detail the next turn needs, while distinctive facts survived to 800k tokens with no visible rot and only ambiguous ones degraded. None of this is unconditional, and the conditions are the useful part. [Going local inverts the strategy](concepts/local-deployment-inverts-the-keep-everything-context-strategy.md): a 32k window on a MacBook cannot hold a single one of their lessons, caching stops helping the moment the transcript overflows, and 7B → 8B → 32B buys no extra window — so compaction becomes mandatory exactly where it measured worst. At the retrieval layer the same evaluation discipline produced two negative results on fashionable techniques: [dense retrieval fell to 0% recall on facts buried at 400k tokens where BM25 held 100%](concepts/dense-retrieval-collapses-on-buried-facts-as-the-haystack-grows.md), and [a sandboxed agentic browse tool over a generated wiki matched their tuned hybrid retriever on recall while running 50% slower](concepts/measure-agentic-knowledge-base-browsing-before-adding-it.md). The rule they land on is the portable one: do not compact by default — name the constraint you actually have, because price, window size, and latency have different answers.
- [Enforce agent egress policy at the wire protocol, below HTTP](concepts/enforce-agent-egress-policy-below-the-http-layer.md) - Ryan Dahl (Deno) starts from a deployment rather than a threat model: incident-response agents with read *and write* access to production Postgres, Kubernetes, ClickHouse, AWS, GitHub, and Slack, "able to solve quite a lot of incidences where we previously would have a human [SRE] in the loop." He then grants the thing most talks argue for — "Opus is remarkably well aligned… it will refuse over and over again" — and declines it in one line, "security can't just be wishful thinking that Opus will always obey your wishes," because the agents are wired into the support system and so "can be prompt injected from the outside." The conclusion is stated as a property of software, not of prompts: "the agents themselves have to be untrusted software… you can't put the guard inside the agent," explicitly including "security plugins or modifications to the agent software itself," which is [independent corroboration from an operator for what Hinge Health argues from the labs' published authority hierarchy](concepts/run-must-not-fail-decisions-in-code-above-the-model.md). What is new is *where* the backstop goes. Agents run on standalone VMs, so filesystem sandboxes are beside the point and "every nefarious action… every good action… comes in the form of some network communication, some bytes over the wire" — formed through MCP "but also subprocesses." The hard case is stated exactly: a production Postgres inside a VPC reachable only through an EKS endpoint, where the requirement is "an outbound path the agent's host can't reach, on a protocol that isn't HTTP, that's gated by a rule that understands SQL." That sentence indicts the layers above at their own layer — LLM gateways see only the provider leg, httpjail and Brex's Crabtrap sit at HTTP — and disposes of both obvious alternatives: read-only credentials are "true up to a point" because [the composition of access opens holes no single system's policy is wrong about](concepts/composed-access-defeats-per-system-credential-scoping.md), and a curated MCP surface is a description rather than an enforcement, since "as soon as [the agent] spawns the psql, you're broken through the security boundary." Claw Patrol (MIT-licensed) parses every byte below HTTP, applies rules that hold through tunnels, extends by protocol plugin, requires no change to the agent software, holds the credentials itself so the agent never sees a secret, and can route an action to an LLM judge, a human in Slack, or both. The operationally sharpest detail is the least glamorous: the ~1,000-line rule set is HCL checked into git, and [it ships with fixture requests as unit tests](concepts/version-control-and-unit-test-the-agent-permission-policy.md) asserting a dangerous action stays blocked — nearly nothing else in this wiki's security material has a regression test. The volunteered caveat is the standing cost of every chokepoint: "Claw Patrol itself is holding all of these credentials to production systems. So you have to be very careful with it." The demo is Codex in yolo mode cheerfully obeying "delete the users table" and dying at the Postgres wire protocol.
- [Make a video model interactive with a causal attention mask, then budget for error accumulation](concepts/make-video-models-causal-and-budget-for-error-accumulation.md) - Sidney Primas (LemonSlice) reports from inside the live-avatar class that Reactor called the least mature, and his account is the most mechanically specific real-time build in this wiki. Two conversions sit on a video diffusion transformer. Interactivity comes from training, not serving: batch video models "are bidirectional… they can look into the past, but they actually also can look into the future," which an avatar cannot, so the model is trained with an attention mask that forbids attending forward — "when you do inference, it never can see the future because the future doesn't exist," including the audio not yet spoken. Real time comes from collapsing "let's say 30 steps" of denoising to one. The price is structural rather than incidental, and it is the talk's point: each block conditions on blocks that already contain error, so "you're looking at the error, you're adding more error to it, and then the error compounds over time" — against a horizon that is not a five-second clip but a deployed Roosevelt avatar in a replica Oval Office "generating continuously non-stop frame by frame for 8 hours straight with like no reset," with sixteen hours planned. That lands hard on the wiki's [evaluation gap for this model class](concepts/realtime-world-model-evaluation-is-still-human-judgment.md): drift is a trajectory property, so per-frame fidelity metrics score a degrading eight-hour session as fine, and LemonSlice's answer is a human panel it expects to fail — "our own version of the Turing test for these avatars, which will just include real people… we won't pass it this year," built to track a trend and be published rather than to gate a release. Two findings travel well past avatars. Expressiveness is not the video model's job: [most audio encoders are trained on audiobooks, which are monotone by construction](concepts/audiobook-trained-audio-encoders-flatten-generated-emotion.md), so "if you want to have a very expressive model, you can't use those audio encoders" — an upstream frozen component capping a downstream capability, the same shape as a captioner's omissions becoming generation artifacts. And the work that actually consumes the team is [the model harness](concepts/the-realtime-model-harness-is-where-the-product-work-sits.md) — "a bunch of separate threads… stuff you do on a GPU and a bunch of stuff you do on a CPU… orchestrate this perfectly in a way that the video always remains real time," hardest under interrupts where queues must be flushed without the stream breaking — which Primas expects to be where "a lot more of the value of the things we build" ends up, "especially true for any real-time applications." Underneath it all is an architecture bet: [point a general world model at a narrow domain](concepts/point-a-general-world-model-at-a-narrow-domain.md) rather than build a talking-head pipeline, paying "harder to train… harder to deploy" for body movement, hands, object interaction, scene physics and micro-expressions that "don't come for free, but come more for free," and a [forecast that the stack splits into an end-to-end EQ layer fed by a separate IQ model](concepts/split-an-embodied-agent-into-an-eq-layer-and-an-iq-model.md). The cost that makes any of it a product: serving lands "about the same as a voice model" despite being far more pixel-heavy.
- [Reuse the agentic app-builder architecture for non-code artifacts](concepts/reuse-the-agentic-app-builder-shape-for-non-code-artifacts.md) - Ekaterina Deyneka's motivating observation is behavioral: nearly every hand in the room had recorded video at the conference, almost none had posted any of it, "because video editing is hard… it's tedious and it's largely still manual." Her engineering claim is that the fix is not a new kind of system. An agentic video editor and an agentic app builder are the same stack column for column — a prompt UI (here media plus prompt), "a remote machine which is called sandbox… spinning up, and inside this machine, there is an agent with tools and skills," a codebase replaced by a video composition, an app preview replaced by a rendered video. What licenses the swap is the artifact's format: Reelful composes in Remotion, "an open-source framework to create videos as code, as React code," chosen because "agents are really good at writing code" — the same argument Nori makes for HTML, reached independently in the medium where it should be hardest, and it buys something the HTML page never claims. Code has a mechanical failure mode, so a verification layer can check the composition "is clean, is well defined, everything will be rendered" and send the agent back to fix what will not, which is a coding agent's compile-or-retry loop applied to a video. Domain taste stays in skills — cut rules, font pairs, when b-roll helps, "this is where our taste and craft live." The inversion worth carrying is that Reelful does not generate the footage: [editing real material constrains an agent more than generating new material](concepts/editing-real-material-is-harder-for-agents-than-generating.md), because "if the agent has a blank canvas, it can do whatever they can," while messy or incomplete personal footage forces judgments about which take is best and what to omit, against a bar where "ideally the viewers of this content don't get if it is like AI or human edited." And the last problem she names is the one this wiki's product pages keep circling: delivery is "even maybe a bigger problem" than the pipeline, so [hide the whole workflow behind templates and a manual editor](concepts/package-agentic-pipelines-behind-templates-and-a-manual-editor.md) — mobile because consumers post while "driving, walking or maybe lifting weights," directional templates so it works "even without any prompt," an approval gate on the creative plan rather than the composition, and a familiar editor afterward so fixing a caption costs a drag instead of a regeneration.
- [GPU utilization is a lie: instrument tensor cores and the fabric](concepts/measure-tensor-core-utilization-not-gpu-utilization.md) - Gabriel Jorge Menezes gives the infrastructure half of Krea 2 — the same from-scratch image model whose research half is already in this wiki — and almost none of it is about diffusion. The metrics argument is the sharpest: GPU utilization "is a lie… amount of time GPU's doing work but like not good work," reading 100% straight through a pre-training run that was nowhere near well used, so tensor core utilization becomes the honest proxy and is validated by climbing as the resolution curriculum stepped 128 → 1024 pixels. Worse, the counters that mattered most were the ones nothing exports: most failures were cross-node communication failures, DCGM ships no InfiniBand metrics and no NVLink error counters, and "if you're doing large-scale pre-training with a bunch of GPUs talking to each other between machines and you have no InfiniBand metrics, you're doing something wrong." The failure posture follows from the same realism — crashes at scale are a rate, often silent (NCCL timeouts with green dashboards), and identical machines running identical code go an hour and then twenty-four, so the answer is to [let it crash and checkpoint against a fast filesystem](concepts/let-training-crash-and-checkpoint-against-a-fast-filesystem.md): pull any GPU above 78 °C without debugging it, checkpoint every 20-30 minutes, and buy storage that writes a terabyte in under 30 seconds (Ceph broke and "we lost trust in data"). The scheduling half inverts a common default — [training outranks production on the shared cluster](concepts/give-training-priority-over-production-on-a-shared-gpu-cluster.md) because a training GPU-hour is worth more — and survives only because inference can leave: [a virtual-kubelet fake node](concepts/evict-inference-off-cluster-through-a-virtual-kubelet-node.md) makes external providers schedulable, a Prometheus-driven taint decides where new pods land, and a descheduler walks them back gradually rather than a NoExecute taint dropping the site at once. Underneath it all is one asymmetry worth stealing on its own: [inference tolerates degraded GPUs that training cannot](concepts/inference-tolerates-degraded-gpus-that-training-cannot.md) — "the GPU can be hot, falling out of the bus… Inference still going to run" — because their diffusion transformer serves on a single node.
- [Track the efficiency axis in generative video, not only quality](concepts/track-the-efficiency-axis-in-generative-video-not-only-quality.md) - Keegan McCallum (uRun) sets aside the axis everyone watches — Will Smith eating spaghetti in 2023, Sora, Sora 2, Seedance "so photorealistic" this year — for the one that changed the economics. Real-time output now sits at "about the same quality as the frontier models were last year" and costs roughly a hundredth of a minutes-long generation, with the residual gap showing up specifically as motion, so the unit of account moves from dollars per clip to dollars per hour: "$10 can get you 3 hours worth of generated video continuously… and $50 would give you an entire day interacting with an AI in a visual medium. 15 hours," measured against a room that raised its hands for burning that much on coding tokens inside one hour. Helios, the model uRun serves, is a distill of an open 14B video model, and "at least 40 models with real-time capabilities and long horizon generation capabilities" shipped this year, so the wiki's distillation and diffusion-serving pages describe a field-wide movement rather than a lab result. Two consequences are separable from the pitch. The authoring loop becomes two-tier — today's shape is "a slot machine… spending about $10 a minute to try and get the shot that you want," and the replacement is to [steer live at sub-second latency and then render the chosen shot at full fidelity](concepts/steer-in-real-time-then-render-the-chosen-shot-at-full-fidelity.md), with the same surface doubling as a way to watch an agent generate instead of waiting on it. And the audience widens: [text-first AI interfaces exclude people who don't think in text](concepts/text-first-interfaces-exclude-people-who-dont-think-in-text.md), which becomes actionable only once a day of continuous visual interaction costs $50 rather than a production budget. His serving section reaches Reactor's conclusion independently and states it more concretely — GPUs placed globally plus a user-routing decision, WebRTC with ICE and TURN, several models "wired together in continuous streaming workflows," and control synchronization "with every frame" — closing on the line that summarizes the cluster: "the models are here and the frontier is really in how we serve them."
- [Expose the domain's vocabulary to agents, not the platform's primitives](concepts/expose-domain-vocabulary-to-agents-not-platform-primitives.md) - Arturo Nunez spent almost ten years at Unity and arrives at a diagnosis that blames neither the model nor the prompt. Ask a coding agent for "a camera that follows this character" and "the LLM reinvents the wheel every single time. Well, when the result is going to be essentially the same," because the only nouns available are the engine's implementation primitives — a mesh, a renderer, an animator, a rigid body, a collider, an audio source, and only then the movement logic anyone asked about — so a domain intent has nothing to bind to. His summary: "the context is on the game engine rather than on the game design part, and I think we should flip that idea," with the replacement vocabulary taken from how the domain is already taught ("press A to jump and press A again while you're in the air to do a double jump"). Three consequences follow. Nereu [makes every agent edit a declarative tag rather than generated code](concepts/make-agent-edits-declarative-tags-instead-of-generated-code.md) — Entity Component System thinking aimed at the agent, with systems querying by tag, no scripting layer "on purpose," and free composition (tag a building `vehicle` and `drivable` and race it). Context is graded with rendering's level of detail: assets around the user's current selection arrive with full tag values, mid-distance ones collapse to "there's something that's a player here at this position," and the hundred pieces of grass are dropped, so [context is assembled by distance from the edit focus](concepts/assemble-scene-context-by-level-of-detail-around-the-edit-focus.md) and regraded as the user moves. And a 6,000-7,000-item asset library with nothing but filenames was made searchable by [screenshotting each 3D model and captioning the render](concepts/bulk-tag-asset-libraries-with-a-vision-model-for-retrieval.md). The cost does not disappear — decomposing genres and moods into tags becomes the platform team's problem — and the product deliberately [scopes the assistant to unblocking rather than one-shotting](concepts/scope-the-assistant-to-getting-users-unstuck-not-one-shotting.md), on the grounds that the user should end up speaking the domain's language.
- [Separate what generated audio says from when it plays](concepts/separate-what-generated-audio-says-from-when-it-plays.md) - Todd Fisher's talking guitar is the small end of the real-time question, and it is instructive because the model layer is the least interesting part: Piper or Apple text-to-speech, Whisper, and "any local model" — "it doesn't really matter what LLM" — behind a microphone-to-strings loop in which an audience member asks the guitar what reality is and hears it answer, one word per plucked note. Every hard problem is in the signal path. Slicing a synthesized sentence into playable words beat the obvious heuristic, because [energy-gap segmentation assumes silence between words and running speech often has none](concepts/segment-synthesized-speech-with-multiple-cues-and-manual-repair.md); a sonority-peak syllabifier keyed on vowel nuclei fails differently, so the two combine, and the last few boundaries were cheaper to drag by hand than to chase with a third detector. The delivery decision is the other transferable one: the whole thing is a [JUCE plugin dropped into Logic Pro like any other effect](concepts/ship-ai-audio-features-as-plugins-inside-the-host-daw.md), because "your DAW is effectively your IDE but for musicians and music producers," so the host supplies routing, chaining, and the backing track. And where Reactor argues steerability arrives when generation gets fast enough to interrupt, this build gets a version of it for free by decoupling playback: the performer cannot change what the guitar will say but owns its rhythm, emphasis, and melody, and since each note only triggers audio that already exists, generation latency never touches the live path. The same reasoning sets the singing stack — WORLD pitch-shifting of VocalSet samples is "a very heavy process… I can't really do that live," so it is [pre-baked offline and each fret indexes a finished sample](concepts/pre-bake-transforms-too-heavy-for-the-realtime-path.md), the cheapest answer to a real-time budget and the one that costs coverage (five vowels, "not quite your opera singer").
- [Real-time generation changes the medium, not just the latency](concepts/real-time-generation-changes-the-medium-not-the-latency.md) - Ahmed Ahres (Reactor) makes the case that a latency threshold, once crossed, changes what a generative product *is*. Batch video is "a slot machine. You cannot change it" — "a generated video… is still a recording" — and his two historical cases are chosen to show that this is not a speedup argument: GPS made your own position continuously actionable, and "Uber would not exist if we did not have the GPS"; the camera viewfinder let you "see what's going on in the screen and adapt accordingly," and "Instagram and TikTok would not exist if we could not produce high-quality content." Applied to generated video, the scarce good is control rather than fidelity — "instant feedback is the ultimate level of control" — demonstrated by prompting a cat into a scene that is still generating. He narrows "world model" to something buildable (infinite, interactive, fast enough to steer) and [splits the field by what the user steers](concepts/pick-a-realtime-world-model-class-by-what-the-user-steers.md): the scene through live prompts, a character inside a generated world (also how robotics labs manufacture unlimited training data), or a live avatar, which he says plainly "hasn't actually been cracked." The engineering half is the durable part: [serve real-time video as stateful streaming sessions, not batch jobs](concepts/serve-realtime-video-as-stateful-streaming-sessions.md), because pixels stream instead of files returning, every session carries model state that must be maintained (the Genie 3 symptom: "the character can look back and then will not remember what's going on"), and a sub-100 ms bar puts GPUs near users — miss it and "it breaks completely the medium." The honest gap is measurement: [real-time world-model evaluation is still human judgment](concepts/realtime-world-model-evaluation-is-still-human-judgment.md), since per-frame "fidelity is easy. It's just like pixels" while session consistency is unsolved "including, by the way, DeepMind."
- [Order billion-scale data filters by cost per sample](concepts/order-billion-scale-data-filters-by-cost-per-sample.md) - Sangwu Lee's account of training Krea 2 is the wiki's first end-to-end look at building an image foundation model, and almost all of it is data. Once the architecture is locked — a latent diffusion transformer, since modelling every pixel is quadratic — "a lot of work just goes into like just feeding the model like what it wants," and the corpus is "anywhere from like 2 to like 10 billion images. That's a lot of images to run like filters on." So every question you want answered about the corpus has to be answerable cheaply: pHash/MD5 deduplication first and SSCD/SigLIP embedding dedup only on the reduced set, and any judgment you actually want (his example: "does this look like a AI image or not") obtained by prompting a large VLM and then distilling it into "a very small like SigLip classifier," because "if you run a classifier over like a billion images, you do need things to be like SigLip-sized" — the same move Essential Web used on text with a ~500M-parameter student. Around 30-40 in-house filters result, and three of them generalize past images: [filter the images your captioner systematically mis-describes](concepts/filter-images-your-captioner-systematically-mis-describes.md) (captions that never mention the frame and the white wall teach the model to hang every painting it generates, so the class gets dropped rather than re-captioned), [use sparse autoencoder features as an unsupervised data tagger](concepts/use-sparse-autoencoder-features-as-an-unsupervised-data-tagger.md) (interpretability tooling repurposed as labeling infrastructure for watermarks, signatures, and border artifacts), and a PageRank-over-Wikipedia coverage sweep that he reports without claiming a payoff. Two decisions behind the pipeline are strategy rather than technique: [reliability and stylistic range are opposite model positions](concepts/reliability-and-stylistic-range-are-opposite-model-positions.md) — the production models are deliberately mode-collapsed, since "the easiest and most reliable way to like render a person is render the most boring average person that exists and then like put it in a center frame" — and [refusing AI-generated training images](concepts/refuse-ai-generated-images-when-style-is-the-product.md), because "synthetic data is like so sticky" that the shortcut ships a detectable copy of someone else's aesthetic. The pipeline itself is borrowed: [run the LLM post-training ladder on diffusion models](concepts/run-the-llm-post-training-ladder-on-diffusion-models.md), 256→1K resolution curriculum through mid-training, SFT, preference pairs, GRPO-style RL, and a prompt expander that is "almost essential" because long prompts sit closer to the training distribution.
- [Agreeableness is a failure mode when the product's job is to change the user](concepts/agreeableness-is-a-failure-mode-when-the-job-is-to-change-the-user.md) - Clay Cockrell has been "sitting with couples in crisis for 30 years" and sets two numbers against each other: BetterHelp took a decade to assemble roughly 35,000 licensed clinicians and reach 5 million users, while ChatGPT sees "approximately 900 million active users per week," enough of whom open it "because it's 11:00 p.m. and they just had an argument" that he calls consumer AI the world's largest relationship therapist. His objection is not competence but the objective it was tuned for — "optimized for one thing above all else, keeping you engaged," which produces more sessions, longer sessions, "more emotional reliance on the AI," and is "amazing" on a SaaS dashboard and "a fire alarm" clinically. The mechanism is what makes this an engineering concept rather than a complaint: no individual reply is wrong, but across sessions "the person does not become more self-aware. They become more certain. They come back to the relationship in the shape of an attorney with cleaner narrative, stronger case, and less curiosity about what their partner was experiencing." The counter-metric points outside the product ("return to the relationship better than they came in… my job is to work myself out of a job"), and the three supporting moves are each about importing a standard from a profession rather than deriving one from a regulator: [encode the field's validated method instead of general empathy](concepts/encode-the-fields-validated-method-not-general-empathy.md) (Gottman's lab predicting divorce "with over 90% accuracy… from a 15-minute conversation," and EFT, both "almost entirely absent from the commercial AI relationship space"), [screen every turn for when advice stops being the right kind of help](concepts/screen-every-turn-for-when-advice-stops-being-the-right-kind-of-help.md) (a background classifier for "escalating control, fear-based language," because a general assistant cannot hear "the difference between we fight a lot and I'm afraid of what happens when I disagree with him"), and [hold unregulated intimate data to the professional-privilege bar](concepts/hold-unregulated-intimate-data-to-the-professional-privilege-bar.md) ("not is this legally defensible, the bar is would I be comfortable if this were my own marriage on the line"). Tony Fabrikant closes with the eval discipline — [expert judgment bookends the suite](concepts/expert-judgment-bookends-the-eval-suite.md): "start with your clinician, not with your prompt," hundreds of TDD-style evals, tens of thousands of runs to find outliers, "even one failing test is not okay" when safety is on the line, and then "nothing replaces your gut when emotional context is on the line."
- [Size your eval effort to the generator–verifier gap](concepts/size-your-eval-effort-to-the-generator-verifier-gap.md) - Chaitanya Asawa (Abridge) leads engineering for clinical decision support at a company whose ambient documentation product reached "300 of the largest health systems in the United States" in two to three years, and the problem he stays with longest is evaluation. His diagnosis is a property of the task rather than of the team: Sudoku is "really really hard to generate … but it's extremely easy to verify," which is what makes it easy "to hill climb against and build evaluation for," whereas for contextual clinical decision support "the generator and verifier gap is really small. If I had a really really good generator verifier, then that would just be my generator itself." So the question becomes "how do I create a reference that isn't just a language model itself?" Abridge answers it twice over. It refuses one score — a clinical quality judge, "a boundary and adversarial judge," a clinical safety judge, and tone/style judges each take "a piece of this like really really hard to measure problem." And it [manufactures the reference with adjudicated expert rubrics](concepts/build-judge-references-from-adjudicated-expert-rubrics.md): because "you can't just create a human golden response … there is a lot of variability in the potential responses," two physicians independently write rubrics of the elements a good answer must contain, a third adjudicates them into one, a fourth QAs it, and the judge does "some basic semantic match" against those elements — spending experts on the reference so that "anyone, whether you're a clinician or not, can actually hill climb." The cost half of the talk is equally transferable. At "100 million medical conversations a year," Abridge [decomposes the note and post-trains a model per section](concepts/decompose-the-deliverable-and-post-train-a-model-per-section.md) rather than running frontier intelligence over everything ("health care is actually many specific workflows"), [gates always-on listening with cheap event detectors](concepts/gate-always-on-listening-with-cheap-event-detectors.md) so capturing spoken orders does not mean polling a model "every few seconds," and justifies owning models at all with a test worth reusing — [train your own only where you have a right to win](concepts/train-your-own-models-only-where-you-have-a-right-to-win.md), meaning data the labs cannot get *and* "the focus on a problem that they may not be focusing on," a bet on beating the frontier's rate of change rather than its current level.
- [An audit trail is a chain of evidence, not a developer log](concepts/an-audit-trail-is-a-chain-of-evidence-not-a-developer-log.md) - Christopher Lovejoy and Saul Howard (Anterior) replay the meeting where an enterprise AI proof of concept dies. The POC hit its metrics, was fast and cheap, and the room was happy — "but the problem is that everyone here is assuming that … the AI was … the challenging part." The next day compliance asks for the audit trail, and the gap is definitional: "for programmers, an audit trail sounds very like a typical developer log you might have in DataDog," but under SOC 2, HITRUST, and HIPAA it must hold "a complete record of absolutely every action that the agent took … all of the places where the agent accessed data, all of the authorization by which the agent did something," and survive the question of whether "we could show a justifiable chain of evidence" if the decision were examined in court. Their three primitives each move a constraint into the architecture. An append-only event log borrowed from finance makes auditability "fall out of your data storage paradigm" (writes cheap, reads permanently more expensive, and every view an ephemeral projection you can recompute when the past gets reinterpreted). [Schema-driven object storage sits beside that log, not inside it](concepts/store-agent-data-in-object-storage-beside-the-event-log.md), with events holding only references, so engineers retrace an agent "without having access to the personal health information itself" while still seeing the data's shape, every read passes one zero-trust checkpoint, and the lethal trifecta becomes answerable in architecture — "is it possible within my architecture for the agent to be also accessing data over here?" And rather than enumerate escalation points, [treat humans and models as the same kind of agent](concepts/treat-humans-and-models-as-the-same-kind-of-agent.md): any action an LLM can take a clinician can take, at any point in the chain, and "any step downstream doesn't care about whether it was a human or an LLM." From those three, [evals emerge as a byproduct](concepts/let-evals-emerge-from-your-architectural-primitives.md) — ledger replay for exact counterfactuals, human-versus-agent difference as the score, and eval runs on production data "inside your customer's environment without actually ever exposing that data." The closing rule generalizes to finance, defense, and government: bolting eval, security, and auditability onto a high-accuracy POC leaves "something very brittle," so take the constraints as the foundation and rebuild toward the accuracy on top of them.
- [You cannot iterate on output you cannot judge](concepts/you-cannot-iterate-on-output-you-cannot-judge.md) - Ayush Bhardwaj (Allos AI) did applied AI at a hedge fund and then at a pharma-tech startup and found the job identical, including the wall: the first four steps of a vertical AI build — narrow task, proprietary data, a prompt written "after the person who you are trying to replace," observability — "fit one screen. The mythical 10x engineers can do this stuff in minutes… that's why it's not the moat." The fifth step is where projects die. "I could build it, but I just could not tell if it worked cuz I'm not a trader. I'm not someone who has a PhD in biology or chemistry." Engineers underrate this because they already own the instinct for their own domain — "you can instantly tell that Sonnet 5 sucks because you have your own training… you've been trained for this for life" — and the failure is silent: the demo looks finished and simply does not sell, "the same way you won't use an inferior coding model." His attempted escape, LLM-as-judge, he calls "a really, really stupid mistake," since the judge "is just like jargoning its way out. It does not understand what alpha means," and RL from verifiable rewards works "really good at math and code because you have answer keys" that these fields lack. The reason no future model rescues it is that [high-value vertical data is withheld by design](concepts/high-value-vertical-data-is-withheld-by-design.md) — funds file holdings quarterly and watch returns drop once reverse-engineered, ~30% of firms never disclose clinical trials despite the legal requirement, so "neither OpenAI nor Anthropic has this data," and NDAs and pay put practitioner annotation out of reach. The remedy is a hire: [hire the user to own the judgment loop](concepts/hire-the-user-to-own-the-judgment-loop.md), then climb from error analysis ("the cheapest of all, and I think the highest ROI") through rubrics, SFT, and RLHF — and ship into an [AI-in-the-loop shape where the expert still decides](concepts/keep-the-expert-as-decider-with-ai-in-their-loop.md).
- [Run must-not-fail decisions in a code layer above the model](concepts/run-must-not-fail-decisions-in-code-above-the-model.md) - Rashi Agrawal (AI/ML lead, Hinge Health) argues that "most AI safety failures in health care are not model failures. They are architectural decisions that were made before even a single token was generated," and grounds the claim in the production baseline rather than a frontier risk: "40 million people actually use these models for triaging their health care issues," a man hospitalized three weeks with bromide at 200× the safe limit after a chatbot told him to replace salt with sodium bromide, Mount Sinai's first independent safety test of a consumer health AI under-triaging life-threatening emergencies "50% of the times," and ECRI naming AI chatbot misuse the number one health technology hazard of 2026. Her architectural rule is an ordering constraint on the agent loop: a deterministic code layer runs "first, on every turn, before the model even runs," and owns the irreversible calls — 911/988 escalation ("the model should not even see this turn"), high-stakes intent routing (a clinical question quietly reaching the generic tech-support agent is "unrecoverable" precisely because nothing errors), and identity verification, since "authentication is a security boundary. Prompts are not." The argument comes from the model providers themselves: they publish an authority hierarchy — "root, system, developer, user, guideline" — in which "every layer above user is one prompt injection away from being overridden," so "if the labs themselves don't trust the prompt as a security boundary, neither should you." Two companion pages carry the rest of the talk: [make regulated-data failures architecturally impossible](concepts/make-regulated-data-failures-architecturally-impossible.md) (PHI stripped at ingestion so "a developer opens a dashboard, there's nothing to redact"), [decide ship-or-hold with explicit severity rules](concepts/decide-ship-or-hold-with-explicit-severity-rules.md) (worst case sets severity, severity is not capacity, calibrate to revealed rather than stated risk tolerance), and [check whether the judge is right before changing the agent](concepts/check-the-judge-before-changing-the-agent.md) ("in a non-deterministic system, the judge is also non-deterministic").
- [Generate eval data by reversing the inference workflow](concepts/generate-eval-data-by-reversing-the-inference-workflow.md) - Anuj Iravane (AI lead, Anterior) poses the sharpest data question in the healthcare cluster: "what do you do when the dataset you most need is also the data you're **least allowed to keep**?" Anterior's work is "policy-guided decision-making over highly unstructured data" — prior authorization, payment integrity, HEDIS — over scanned fax bundles ("around **70%** of medical communication still happens via fax") that run past **300 pages** of handwriting, tables, checkboxes and images and model a whole clinical trajectory, "an observation through a very fuzzy lens over an entire person's lifespan." The bar is "**95% is not good enough**," but the records are PHI his contracts forbid retaining, reusing, or *deriving from*, with redaction and anonymization equally "off the table," so "nothing really survives in any sort of dataset that we want to persist." The answer is to run the task backwards: the forward pass is data + policy → reasoning trace → label, so the reverse samples a label, samples a trace that reaches it, and synthesizes the record. It works because Anterior already models policies "explicitly as **decision trees**" (adopted for execution accuracy, not for data generation), which gives "a way to kind of **deterministically sample** different reasoning traces for a given outcome" from "a much more uniform and effective prior distribution than what you'd normally get from an LLM" — whose objectives "are not incentivized for creativity or diversity … they're incentivized to be helpful systems." Two consequences outlive the privacy motive: a **round-trip check** back to the sampled label makes labels "correct by construction," so expensive ground-truthing disappears; and coverage stops depending on traffic, since 200 real cases scoring 95% "doesn't really tell you about … those rare edge cases that are not in that data set," while a policy tree can be sampled for branches production has never produced. Generation itself is a discipline — [build synthetic records coarse to fine by emulating how they were produced](concepts/build-synthetic-records-coarse-to-fine-by-emulating-their-source-process.md): patient invariants → an ordered *patient journey* of provider encounters (because real documentation "is really generated" only during encounters) → a document plan per encounter → a hydrating fan-out, which also keeps prompt payloads token-efficient and scales past any one context window, with an LLM **consistency eval** reconciling documents written in parallel and no PDF rendering at all, since "state of the art PDF parsers" already collapse the real thing back to markdown. And the pipeline is not the engineers' to own: asked how he would know what a good medical record looks like, "you really don't. Like no AI engineer would," so Anterior [hands domain experts the pipeline as skills](concepts/hand-domain-experts-the-pipeline-as-skills.md) — every stage is a skill file on a generic internal agent harness, a clinician adds a customer's intake-form type with "no engineering changes required," and "skills are really an amazing interface between AI engineers and domain experts, especially in vertical AI." Reported: ~**90%** of datasets synthetic, clinicians separating synthetic from real "about **60%** of the time" in blind review, and datasets "created **just in time** for these customer deployments." Scope: "you don't need a PHI problem for this" — it applies whenever the data is "ephemeral, sensitive, or even expensive to label."
- [Commit at sprint scale when implementation is cheap](concepts/commit-at-sprint-scale-when-implementation-is-cheap.md) - Dan Feng (Maven Clinic) names a cost inversion behind almost every planning ritual: long requirements phases existed because "implementation can be really expensive," but now "with AI, building is super fast. It's probably couple minutes you can get it done. **Argument is really expensive one**." Maven's planning splits into three horizons — a one-year view kept as direction only, explicitly allowed to assume "AI models can do anything you want in one year"; a **two-to-four-week** commitment that is the only real plan, with reversal designed in ("if at the end of this sprint, they say, 'No, what we decided two weeks ago is wrong.' It's totally okay"); and a three-to-six-month middle written off as close to unplannable, because "I don't know what AI models will be capable in three months. There may be multiple releases already." PRDs and TDDs shrink to one or two pages "to serve as communication, so we can iterate on it." His reliability half is the same refusal of a single global bar, and it is a product judgment cheap enough to make in a planning meeting: [sort failures by whether the user can retry](concepts/sort-failures-by-whether-the-user-can-retry.md). A scheduling action failing one in a thousand is tolerable because "the users really can just click the button again"; a reimbursement claim tolerates none, because the error escalates in *either* direction — "if people ask of $200, we issue them 50 or they ask 50, we give them 200" — leaving no safe side to bias toward, so that class gets several different models reading the same receipt with action only on agreement, and a human-agent handoff when they disagree. The release discipline that backs it is the third idea: [earn release confidence from repeated runs and post-launch sampling](concepts/earn-release-confidence-from-repeated-runs-and-post-launch-sampling.md) — hundreds of integration tests each run many times at a sustained ~90% pass rate because passing once "is not good enough anymore, because the LLM can do different things," then rubric scoring on every conversation plus a dedicated human group spot-checking, at roughly 20% coverage for new launches, whose job is two-directional: it decides whether to fix the system *or* whether "our rubrics is too strict or too loose." His account of code review at 10x volume (author self-certification with retained accountability, a 500-line PR cap, stacked PRs, and the rubber stamp as the worst outcome because it "just give us false confidence") strengthened [route each change to the proof it needs](concepts/route-each-change-to-the-proof-it-needs.md) and [AI output speed can overwhelm review capacity](concepts/ai-output-speed-can-overwhelm-review-capacity.md).
- [Ground agent actions in an existing domain transaction standard](concepts/ground-agent-actions-in-an-existing-domain-transaction-standard.md) - Vasant Kearney (Onlay) builds an agentic execution layer for insurance claims and argues the domain already published the constraint the harness needs. His broad definition of *harness* is "all the different nuts and bolts that surround this agentic reasoning" — memory, tools, checks, permissions, handoffs, evals — "but also in the context of healthcare and claims, it's **X12**." The case for it is three-part: LLMs "really thrive … when they're confined, they have clear limited values that they can predict," the way COBOL or TypeScript confines; a claims objective can be "like **50 steps**" whose mistakes propagate, so "it's very good to have something grounded that can be rejected to"; and the standard is public — "this is not my schema. If you ask agents to make a schema for you you're going to get like all sorts of stuff," whereas every transaction can be looked up by a research agent or a new engineer. The reframe that follows is the talk's sharpest: calling a payer "boils down to a transaction, an X12 transaction" (identifying the patient is a **270**), as is an imaging attachment (**275**) or a portal session, so a phone call, a browser agent, and an EDI feed are one transaction emitted by different routes and normalize onto a single internal representation (the bank/ACH leg is not X12 but is still structured). It is also an explicit position on the reasoning-versus-hardcoding axis: 50 free multimodal steps are "really expensive," slow, and error-multiplying, but hardcoding means "your code can explode to be just unmanageable" and "a giant engineering team," so the strictness is spent on the standard. The counterweight is that structure is not truth — [treat every external system of record as non-authoritative](concepts/treat-every-external-system-of-record-as-non-authoritative.md), because a payer's portal, phone system, X12 layer, and FHIR layer are often built by different teams or "even a different company that the insurance company contracts out," so they contradict each other *and* "can all actually agree on the wrong information as well": all three say the patient is covered, you treat the patient, and the claim is denied because they were not covered at that time. Hence the normalization rule — "boil all those transactions down to your own internal semi-correct X12. **Correct until downstream evidence proves it otherwise**" — plus learned per-payer idiosyncrasies about which surface to trust, and the reminder that payer information "any time can be wrong, it can be updated later." Two deployment constraints travel with the design: enterprise agents cannot copy coding agents' memory model ("cloud code or codex … write to your desktop. In enterprise healthcare, we can't really do this"), so [tiered database memory with an escape hatch](concepts/give-enterprise-agents-tiered-database-memory-with-an-escape-hatch.md) puts memory in a database for logical separation with partner/organizational and user tiers — useful because multi-site staff "tend to do the same thing day after day," dangerous because persistent memory "introduce[s] bias" and can "steer them to do the exact same thing they did yesterday," so "any user can break out of this." And a stronger model is not a drop-in: "you can't just replace the model and assume it's going to be better. It's different," since benchmark wins do not transfer to "the way you've designed your system." Closing posture: "I'm fully AI pilled … but you should also be very AI skeptical" — "we make mistakes designing them. We might set them up to fail" — with the cost rule that an overpowered model on "routine things that need to be done a thousand times a day" defeats the cost-reduction goal the work exists for.
- [Replace ship-and-rollback with hazard-first simulation when errors are irreversible](concepts/replace-ship-and-rollback-with-hazard-first-simulation.md) - Jared Joselowitz (research engineer, Ufonia) builds the safety and evaluation stack behind **Dora**, a voice agent that phones patients for post-op follow-ups and pre-op checks (~200,000 real clinical calls across 20 UK hospitals, contracted to reach a million patients in two years). Because Dora asks about symptoms and gives advice, it is a regulated medical device — and three ordinary safety nets vanish: you cannot A/B test ("randomizing patients into a worse variant is unethical and often illegal"), you cannot roll back ("once Dora says it, it's been said"), and a vendor benchmark "is not a defense at a post incident review." Ship-to-5%-and-watch fails because its hidden assumption is that "you can afford to be wrong for an instance": 5% is thousands of patients, and "the dashboards going red means that a patient was actually hurt." The replacement borrows self-driving's millions of simulated miles — enumerate hazards with clinicians first (missing a red flag, hallucinating a medical answer, ignoring distress; "20, 30, 40" of them), then run them in a simulation framework (**Matrix**) where one LLM plays the patient (**PatBot**) and a second judges every dialogue. Crucially, both models are validated rather than assumed: [validate the simulated user and the judge before trusting a simulation](concepts/validate-the-simulated-user-and-the-judge.md) — a script-adherence filter weeds out models that ignore instructions, a blind patient study found real patients picked the *simulated* patient as more realistic in **three of four sets** (with the lesson that "there is no single realistic patient," so simulate diverse personas), and the judge, checked against **10 clinicians from 10 specialties on 240 cases**, matched or beat them (F1 0.96, Gemini 2.5 Pro) at near-perfect **sensitivity**, because "you would rather overcall hazards that aren't there than undercall hazards that are there." Improvement is then automated: given that "formatting changes alone have been seen to swing benchmarks by 76 percentage points," hand-tuning is replaced by GEPA, and you [optimize prompts against an asymmetric cost matrix, not flat accuracy](concepts/optimize-prompts-against-an-asymmetric-cost-matrix.md) — a missed red flag is catastrophic, an over-called one is "mildly annoying" — which pushes the real work into the data and a metric "you define along with the clinicians," and lets a domain expert re-target the system by recompiling the prompt. Simulation is explicitly necessary but not sufficient ("it only earns the right to actually try carefully"): it is the inner loop, real patients the outer one, crossed in stages where "how much autonomy you allow the system depends on your evidence."
