The deliverable is traceability — every call, dataset, pinned prompt, and judge verdict maps back to the exact hazard it addresses, because "you don't ship the model, you ship the evidence." New modalities change the hazards (voice adds backchannels and interruptions that make weak models drop safety advice mid-sentence) but not the method: "voice is just a new module in the same safety case."
- [Run Parallel Specialist Models Behind a Speak-Up Gate](concepts/run-parallel-specialist-models-with-a-speak-up-gate.md) - Vivek Muppalla (head of engineering, Hippocratic AI) walks the production stack behind 200M+ outbound clinical patient calls across 60+ health systems, built to refuse the usual tradeoff between models accurate enough to be clinically safe (tens of seconds to answer) and models fast enough for a phone call. Three durable patterns: 31 models run concurrently on every turn — one holding the conversation plus 30 specialists — with each specialist first making a cheap "do I need to speak?" check that short-circuits, because "a singular model" is "one point of failure"; ASR is a decoder-only audio LLM [conditioned on conversation context and domain vocabulary](concepts/condition-asr-on-conversation-context-and-domain-vocabulary.md) so a drug name resolves against a finite list ("most of what looks like model reasoning failures end up actually being model mishearing things"); and evals are [sized to the error rate the consequence demands](concepts/size-eval-suites-to-the-error-rate-the-consequence-demands.md) — 1% of 10,000 daily calls is 100 misrouted patients, and catching a 1% error rate takes ~450 tests.
- [Layer brand voice into four composable prompt tiers](concepts/layer-brand-voice-into-composable-prompt-tiers.md) - Isadora Martin-Dye (founder, Isadora & Co) runs a 225-year-old Virginia wedding venue and built its AI agents (a couples-facing assistant "In Bloom," a multi-venue version, a personal companion, and Thread Light for families of missing people). Her thesis: a brand voice that survives real users "isn't an instruction you write once — it's an architecture." A single "write in our brand's voice" system prompt holds on the **happy path** but breaks on "**turn 21**," the first case the examples never covered, because one prompt is doing **four completely different jobs**. Fix: a **four-layer stack** composed in a **fixed order through one assembly function** ("hard rules first, task to last," like Google Maps routing). **(1) Immutable identity** — hard constraints "the brand structurally cannot say," overridable by nothing below (mandatory AI disclosure "not if asked, but before they ask"; "you are software, you do not have a body," so "I'd love to show you around" is forbidden but "the team would love to host you for a tour" is fine; Thread Light forbids "confirmed/identified/matched/proven/linked/solved" because "match" to a grieving parent is "the single most damaging thing a product could ever do"). **(2) Situational mode** — real-time who/what-they're-going-through signals injected *before* generation, with tone notes rendered before numeric constraints so reversing them doesn't make the prose "mechanically slotted." **(3) Example-anchored voice** — the tone guide/phrase list, "where most teams start and stop," good on the happy path but "not the right tool for guarantees." **(4) Post-generation veto** — "the only layer that isn't a prompt," which reads the actual output. The load-bearing distinction is that the first three layers are probabilistic instructions ("asking nicely and hoping") that "usually" work but "a prompt will eventually lose," so [gate generated output with a deterministic post-generation veto](concepts/gate-generated-output-with-a-deterministic-veto.md) — "instructions are probabilistic, permission is deterministic." A *soft flag* ("honesty inspector") and a *hard reject* ("numbers guard") check specifics against an allow list ("a date not in the allow list doesn't ship"), justified by cost asymmetry (false positive = a double-checked response; false negative = "a hallucinated number or a privacy violation that ships to a client") and built deterministically as an explicit "determinism over coverage" trade-off. It is needed because "usually" is fine for a slightly off-brand sentence but "not fine when the model is confidently inventing a fact a real person is about to act on — a date, a price, a policy, a promise" (a warm confident offer of a fabricated booked date is "worse than a cold one… a disappointment with a 48-hour delay"). Multi-tenant seam = "calling one function" (Layer 1 shared, Layers 2-3 per-tenant); a silently-defaulted identity field leaked one venue's persona into others, fixed by "identity must never have a default. A missing brand identity is a crash, not a fallback. It must fail loud." Users "aren't testing your brand voice, they are trusting it."
- [Build a file-based research wiki with progressive-disclosure retrieval](concepts/file-based-research-wiki-with-progressive-disclosure-retrieval.md) - Paul Iusztin (Decoding AI) and Louis-François Bouchard (Towards AI) demo an **"AI Research OS"**: a personal memory layer that sits between a "second brain" (10,000+ notes across Obsidian, Readwise, Notion, Google Drive) and any harness (Codex, Claude Code), open-sourced as a Claude Code plugin. Its premise is that with an agent "the context window becomes everything… and when you stop the conversation, it loses everything," so the bottleneck is not how much context you feed but how you *reuse* it. The build deliberately "forgets the infrastructure you think you need" — no vector DB, knowledge graph, or semantic search — in favor of plain Markdown plus a reference index, in three tiers: `raw/` immutable source copies, an `index.yaml` catalog (per-source summaries + metadata; one example held 10 sources and 38 derived pages), and an LLM-generated `wiki/` of derivatives (per-source executive summaries, concepts, entities, comparisons, notes, open questions). Retrieval reads **cheapest-first** — index → source executive summary (often enough, then stop) → wiki derivatives → the raw source only as a last resort — so an agent rarely loads a whole document. The wiki is "alive": every question spawns a logged derivative file, so it compounds from *use*, not only ingestion, while the raw personal vault (Obsidian, organized with Tiago Forte's PARA method) stays an immutable snapshot the LLM never edits. Choosing to build this at all is itself a decision node: [choose the research tool by reuse and ownership, not just speed](concepts/choose-the-research-tool-by-reuse-and-ownership.md) — route a throwaway answer to Google/ChatGPT, a one-off change to Codex/Claude Code, revisitable content to NotebookLM (powerful but you don't own it, not agent-native, weak for coding), production scale to a RAG/vector-DB pipeline (needs infra, not human-inspectable), and a personalized, inspectable, compounding store to a self-owned file wiki. Under the hood, a deep-research loop (evolved from static `research.md` to a second-brain-aimed loop to the +wiki layer) fans out an orchestrator + per-question sub-agents on Gemini grounded in Google, then ranks and fully scrapes only top-K links; a light/fast/deep depth control manages the heavy token cost. By design a builder workflow, not a product — acknowledged gaps are source provenance/freshness ("hard to know which sources are outdated or weak or strong") and better linting + memory compaction.
- [Own the agent loop on a typed effects runtime](concepts/own-the-agent-loop-on-a-typed-effects-runtime.md) - Gabe De Mesa (OpenGov AI Agents team) details the production architecture of **OG Assist**, an agent embedded as a nav-bar button across OpenGov's government ERP products where each product team contributes its own tools and skills. His two load-bearing bets are durable engineering decisions. First, **own the agent loop**: the team started on **LangGraph**, which "was fine until the team really started to scale and our use cases started to evolve," then moved to a self-owned loop built on **Effect-TS** (a functional-effects TypeScript library) so that tracing/spans, structured concurrency, logging, and dependency-injected model hot-swap propagate uniformly across the *whole* loop "out of the box" — "you can't scale what you can't see." The Effect AI package supplies `chat`/`language model` primitives (`stream text` from a prompt), DI lets them swap the underlying model without touching the loop, and tools plug in the same way (define tool → add to a tool kit → register the tool kit with the model). Second, [adopt A2A's spec as an internal front-end/back-end contract](concepts/adopt-a2a-as-an-internal-front-end-back-end-contract.md): they modeled their back-end agent routes and schema on Google's **A2A** agent card (name, description, capabilities) that both front-end and back-end consume and produce — deliberately *internal*, with no remote ownership boundary, because "this rigorous spec really helped drive our development and drive alignment" (A2A extensions like metadata and A2UI let it grow). This qualifies the usual A2A-vs-MCP-by-ownership-boundary heuristic: a rigorous open protocol can earn its place as an internal API contract for spec discipline alone. Surrounding already-covered production practices are reinforced rather than re-conceptualized: thumbs up/down feedback + automated evals in CI against real completions ("shipping is the start, not the finish"), deterministic human-in-the-loop interrupts before mutating tool calls, on-demand ephemeral sandboxes for agent-written code/files, rolling summarization + recall over the summary for long context, runtime generative UI, and internal Claude/Cursor use for developer velocity.
- [Stage agentic-engineering adoption with a delegation maturity model](concepts/stage-agentic-engineering-adoption-with-a-delegation-maturity-model.md) - Angie Jones recounts transforming Block's 3,500-person engineering org into an autonomous one, and her sharpest lesson is that **adoption is not impact**: within months ~90% of engineers used Goose (Block's internal coding agent and the MCP-client reference implementation) and Claude Code, and metrics + token bills proved it, yet "features certainly weren't making it to our customers any faster" because usage was stuck at IDE-level autocomplete and chat (three enablement phases: experimentation, adoption, impact). Her first tool is a **maturity model** (reorganized with help from Steve Yegge's "Gas Town" article) that ranks each engineer by their *delegation/orchestration relationship* with agents, not usage: Stage 0 no AI; Stage 1 autocomplete only, never agent mode; Stage 2 chatting with agents but no PRs; Stage 3 delegate tasks + review output; Stage 4 multiple agents in parallel; Stage 5 ("final boss") delegate complete tasks with shippable results and no human hand-holding — the bulk of 3,500 engineers sat at Stage 1–2 and the target was Stage 5. Her second tool is a leverage strategy: rather than upskill everyone, [drive org-wide adoption through champions and AI-ready repos](concepts/drive-org-wide-agentic-adoption-through-champions-and-ai-ready-repos.md) grounded in the **1-9-90 rule** (1% create, 9% interact, 90% consume) — hand-pick a ~1% **AI champions** cohort (~50 engineers, not volunteers, ≥30% time, resilient to non-deterministic AI) from the most critical repos across every corner of Block (Square, Cash App, Afterpay, Tidal; front/back/mobile/data/infra) and every repo shape, and have them make the *shared* repos **AI-ready** — `AGENTS.md`/`CLAUDE.md` context files, rules-as-guardrails, slash commands + later agent skills, an enabled AI code reviewer, PR attribution, monorepo root-and-service inheritance — so agent delegation "felt native to how people already work" for the 90% (bottom-up per-team convergence beat top-down "AI or die" pressure). She then walks the org up the ladder — hands-off delegation from where requirements live (Jira/Linear/GitHub + `@goose` in Slack, a full bug→3-options→fix→PR cycle in ~5 min; 3-month results AI-authored code +69%, time savings +37%, automated PRs 21×), a **Codex auto-fix review loop** and **isolated cloud workspaces** for Stage-4 parallelism, a **Builder Bot** orchestrator, and a machine-readable **company world model** over ~25,000 repos — reaching Stage 5 where anyone could @Build-A-Bot in Slack. The talk closes on an unresolved caveat: the autonomy push was followed by **layoffs**, leaving open "are we sure this is where we want to end up?"
- [The Fat-Agent tool overload collapses accuracy and inflates latency](concepts/fat-agent-tool-overload-collapses-accuracy-and-latency.md) - Sohail Shaikh and Ankush Rastogi (Prosodica) benchmark the **"Fat Agent"** anti-pattern — loading every tool's name, description, and JSON schema into the system prompt on every request — and show it collapses as the catalog grows: tool-selection accuracy falls ~78% at 10 tools → ~40% at ~100 → **13.6% at 741 tools** (≈1 correct in 8), a 741-tool catalog costs **~127,000 tokens/request** before the user's question, and time-to-first-token past ~500 tools can exceed **5 seconds**. The failure is structural ("it fails because every request is forced to carry the entire catalog," not because one tool is badly written) and rooted in **lost-in-the-middle** — models attend to the beginning and end of long context, so schemas packed in the middle are used unreliably. The fix is a **Semantic Tool Router** = "RAG for tools" + **Just-in-Time Context Injection**: [retrieve tool descriptions before loading large tool catalogs](concepts/retrieve-tool-descriptions-before-loading-large-tool-catalogs.md) by embedding each description offline into a vector index (Chroma/Pinecone/Qdrant), then at runtime embedding the query, retrieving the **top-K (K≈3–5)** closest tools, and injecting only those schemas. The routed agent holds **above ~83%** across the same catalog sizes with near-flat latency and **~99% fewer tool-context tokens** — echoing Anthropic's reported **150k→2k** (98.7%) drop from on-demand MCP tool loading — and the router also *removes* the wrong tools from the model's choice set, cutting cross-tool confusion. Threshold: below ~20 tools a router may be unnecessary (static loading is fine), past ~50 tools in production it pays off, though tool confusion can appear with only a few tools. Tuning is an eval discipline: [tune a tool router with a K-sweep and guard its failure modes](concepts/tune-a-tool-router-with-k-sweep-and-guard-its-failure-modes.md) — measure accuracy, TTFT, input tokens per request, and cost per 1,000 calls over synthetic tool pools scaled to 1,041 tools (plus the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard and Skills-bench-style scenarios); sweep K at 3/5/10 and pick the smallest K meeting the target (K=5 default); and guard three failure modes (router miss → widen K or run a second pass; weak descriptions → weak embeddings; rare tools → monitor misses and rewrite). "If your agent starts failing as tools are added, it doesn't automatically mean your prompts are bad — it may mean the architecture is asking the model to solve the wrong problem." This strengthens the previously AWS-only retrieve-tool-descriptions concept with a second, benchmark-heavy source.
- [Shrink the per-step payload the agent loop re-sends](concepts/shrink-the-per-step-payload-the-agent-loop-re-sends.md) - Erik Hanchett (senior developer advocate, AWS) gives a ~6-min lightning talk with five code-level levers to cut agent token cost after a production agent's bill was "not good… because it was doing too much" — not the model, but re-sent context, expensive-model overuse, and over-long tool loops, each fixable in "a few lines of code" with AWS **Strands** agents (mechanisms are framework-agnostic) and no prompt or model change. The unifying model: total token spend ≈ (payload re-sent per step) × (number of steps), and a naive loop inflates both by re-transmitting the system prompt, tool results, and full conversation history on *every* iteration and by letting a tool call "over and over." Attack the payload factor with three levers — **cache the static prefix** (`cache_prompt = default` sends the system prompt full once and a "much reduced" reference thereafter; tool prompts and messages can be cached too), **offload large tool results** (store locally/in the cloud and keep a summary so the result "isn't added into the context every time the tool loops or every time the agent loops"), and **window the conversation history** (Strands' `SlidingWindowConversationManager` sends only the last ≈10 messages, recovering the lost early context by summarizing older turns into the window). Attack the per-token factor by [routing each request to the cheapest sufficient model by difficulty](concepts/route-each-request-to-the-cheapest-sufficient-model-by-difficulty.md) — a newer frontier model for a very difficult task, Claude Haiku for something simple, Claude Sonnet in between, chosen by an `if` statement or even "another model that's very cheap" acting as a classifier, rather than "the most expensive model for everything." And attack the step-count factor with a **max-iterations cap** on the tool loop (without it a tool can run "10, 20 times… an infinite loop, which would be very bad for your token usage") plus pre-deploy **observability** on each tool's loop count and duration — the cost-hygiene sibling of retry amplification. The two new concepts carve the distinct durable ideas (per-step re-transmission cost model; difficulty-triggered model routing) against a heavily-covered area — KV-cache-hit-rate, agent-swarm-KV-working-sets, context-window-editing, offload-long-horizon-state, frequent-intentional-compaction, verification-guardrails-downshift, abstract-LLM-routing-API, and Sakthivel's input-dominates-cost — cross-referenced rather than duplicated, with the loop cap folded into the existing contain-retry-amplification concept.
- [Cut coding-agent cost by fixing the input, not the model or output](concepts/cut-coding-agent-cost-by-fixing-the-input-not-the-model-or-output.md) - Rajkumar Sakthivel (LLM-infrastructure engineer, Tesco) traces an AI-coding bill that jumped ~£15→£200 in a month with no usage change to oversized **input context**, not "AI thinking" (output): a typical query sent ~45,000 tokens when only ~5,000 mattered, so ~40,000 useless tokens were paid for every query ("like ordering a pizza and paying for extra nine pizzas you don't eat"). His "most important slide" is the cost asymmetry — **~90% of AI-coding cost is input, ~10% is output** (restated as models ≈30% vs context ≈70%) — so cutting output 75% saves only ~8% of the total while cutting input 94% saves ~61%: "fix the input… the model choice matters less than you think." The obvious fixes underdeliver: prompt tweaks ("be short, only show relevant code") change nothing because the 45K tokens are already charged before the model reads the instruction; model settings (max tokens, temperature) change output not input; output compression works but is capped because output is only ~10% of cost. The mechanism is to [insert a local code-index retrieval layer between the codebase and the coding agent](concepts/insert-a-local-code-index-retrieval-layer-between-codebase-and-agent.md) (open-sourced as **Code Context Engine / CCE**) that returns only relevant pieces via a five-step pipeline: AST-aware chunking (functions/classes/methods, not random chunks); *parallel* hybrid search combining semantic-by-meaning and keyword-by-exact-name (semantic misses exact names, keyword misses related ideas like "login flow" vs "sign in"; each misses ~1 in 4 results alone, ~1 in 10 together); compression (a 50-line function → its name + a five-line description); a **call-graph relationship layer** (one hit surfaces connected code across files); and a **relevance gate** that scores every result and drops low ones ("no bad context"). The relevance gate was the hard part — a bad result gives a "confident wrong answer… worse than no answer" — where an LLM self-judge was too slow (+2-3 s/query) and a fixed similarity threshold too crude (short queries score low on perfect matches), so a simple weighted formula (50% meaning + 30% keyword + 20% recency) with an adaptive limit ran in ~0.4 ms with no extra AI calls: "simple formula beats the complex model most of the time." Everything runs locally (nothing to the cloud), and one shared index plus a memory layer serves Claude Code, Cursor, and Copilot so the codebase is "explained once" instead of re-discovered per tool. Honest limits: on FastAPI (53 files, 20 questions) tokens fell 83K→4.9K/question (94%, or 523/question with compression) at ~90% right-code accuracy, and 247 real queries saved 12.4M tokens (~$186), but the headline 94% is worst-case against a full-file baseline (real tools are already smarter, so real savings are lower) and recall "dropped almost zero" on a 396-file mixed codebase — it works when files each do one thing and struggles when files do many.
- [Keep inference off the microcontroller: build the AI-native device as a thin client over a backend](concepts/keep-inference-off-the-microcontroller-thin-device-over-a-backend.md) - Lech Kalinowski (PhD physicist, Callstack) built "Vault," a text-first, dual-display handheld AI terminal — an ESP32-S3 dual-core microcontroller with a keyboard, encoder, a fast emissive OLED "live" surface and a slow bistable e-paper "content" surface, all on one lithium-polymer cell — as a calm, distraction-free remote control for an OpenClaw agent running on his NVIDIA DGX Spark. The durable architecture lesson is **"keep the model off the metal"**: the MCU runs only a tiny fast firmware doing I/O and rendering (fixed static buffers of pre-allocated one-bit images, deliberately *no markdown engine and no `malloc` on the MCU*), while a dependency-free Python backend owns all inference, autonomous agents, and a game engine — serving an open-source ~120B model (gpt-oss:120b) via NVIDIA **TensorRT-LLM** exposed OpenAI-style behind an **LLM proxy** (needed because not every open model matches the OpenAI API shape, so the adapter lets the firmware target one stable interface while the backend model swaps). This resolves the four-axis edge-ownership decision in favor of thin-client-over-backend rather than deploy-small: the 120B model plainly fails the "fits the hardware" axis for the MCU, so the whole model is offloaded and only interaction/rendering stays local. Four modes switch with a single slash command — **shell** (settings, Wi-Fi), **assist**, **control** (drive OpenClaw), and an **LLM-native RPG** — and the device surfaces an agent's thinking and tool calls in real time across the two surfaces; fault tolerance ("bulletproof") falls out of keeping the device thin (OLED down → e-paper, keyboard down → encoder, Wi-Fi down → local shell). The second durable idea is design-level: [design LLM-native interactive experiences around narrative state, not numeric mechanics](concepts/design-llm-native-experiences-around-narrative-state-not-numbers.md) — the RPG drops HP and dice and keeps LLM-tracked mood, NPCs, memory, and generated worlds (rendered as one-bit matrices) as the authoritative state, with the takeaway "try narrative, context matters, and in general not numbers." War stories each cost ~a day (software I2C, a GPIO-13 silent failure, a power regulator killing the fragile OLED/e-paper, a noisy cheap encoder); ~130 commits over ~3 months, framed as "basically a Game Boy" to play with LLMs, with a provisional patent filed for the calm text-first niche.
- [Optimize an agent with a branch-per-hypothesis coding-agent loop](concepts/optimize-an-agent-with-a-branch-per-hypothesis-coding-agent-loop.md) - Alfonso Graziano (tech lead, Nearform; O'Reilly author of *Learning AI Native Software Engineering*) presents a production workflow for using AI to build AI — two agents with different jobs, a **coding agent** (Claude Code) as the *builder* that writes/changes a **product agent's** code, and the product agent feeding back evals and thinking traces so the builder can self-improve — targeting two failure classes: bad performance on **evals** and on **live data**. The eval backbone is a **golden dataset** (input → expected output, where expected output is often "call this tool / with this parameter / in this chain") treated as a test suite for a non-deterministic system, plus **scorers** yielding an accuracy number for baseline/regression tracking. For evals he built **AutoAgent** (inspired by Karpathy's "auto research" loop, where a coding agent mutating ML code/hyperparameters improves a model): it hill-climbs the product agent's evals as one **hypothesis per git branch** (tackle one failure class at a time), keeping the branch when evals improve and **rolling back to the previous branch** on regression, with a global cross-run **memory file**, per-iteration `reports.md`, and a full readable/steerable **change log** of hypotheses — and a hard human-in-the-loop **guardrail** forbidding the coding agent from editing the golden dataset/scorers just to pass ("without actually cheating"). Reported lift: a tool-less Mastra "math agent" **18%→83%** in ~10 iterations, a real production agent **67%→86%**, and **+10%** on an already-human-optimized agent (finding improvements humans hadn't). For live data he runs a second loop: collect production traces (interaction, tool usage, latency, tokens) plus user thumbs/comments or SME trace annotations, then an agent workflow packaged as a **skill** clusters the failures, does an adversarial review + root-cause analysis (the coding agent has code + trace access), and emits a markdown report (clusters, trace IDs, root causes, fixes); humans triage/validate with SMEs (fix-now/later/never, discard false positives/intended behavior) and — the durable step — [promote every validated failure cluster back into the golden dataset](concepts/promote-validated-live-trace-failure-clusters-into-the-golden-dataset.md) and scorers so the fix becomes a permanent regression test ("self-healing evals"), run ~once per sprint. Both loops are enabled by **Harness Engineering**: the spec-driven environment (each failure mode becomes a spec), quality gates (lint, unit tests, evals, LLM code review), context engineering, and observability built around the coding agent so it can change code, validate its own changes, and propose new ones reliably.
- [Shard cache-augmented generation into parallel buckets with a supervisor](concepts/shard-cache-augmented-generation-into-parallel-buckets-with-a-supervisor.md) - Luis Romero-Sevilla (VP of AI, Orbis Operations) walks a retrieval-architecture decision for a hard corpus shape — a large document collection where *every* document is relevant to a set of global questions, the documents are deeply interconnected, and the whole collection becomes obsolete and is replaced with new information very fast — and shows why each standard approach breaks on some axis of it. **Simple RAG** (vector DB + embedding model) inserts fast, so replacing an obsolete collection is cheap, but similarity-threshold retrieval cannot select a subset when all documents matter (and you can't dump the whole collection into the LLM). **GraphRAG** (LLM extracts entities/relationships → knowledge graph → navigate to synthesize a cross-collection answer) is excellent for rarely-changing corpora but recomputing the graph on every replacement is computationally very expensive and slow. **CAG (Cache Augmented Generation)** notes that each document has to pass through an LLM for entity/relationship structure anyway, so instead load all documents into a large-context model and cache the context by storing the model's KV matrix — but a limited context window means overfilling it degrades answer quality. **ECAG (Extended CAG)** is the fix: run many CAG caches in parallel, sharding documents across "context buckets" so each cache answers questions about its own content, and put a smarter **supervisor** model on top that asks the right questions to the right buckets, explores them to progressively build understanding, asks specific buckets follow-up questions when something looks interesting, and synthesizes — faster than GraphRAG (caches load in parallel, no graph recompute) and more accurate than simple RAG. A non-obvious bucketing rule falls out of the interconnectedness: [distributing documents by domain backfires](concepts/distribute-documents-across-cache-buckets-in-no-particular-order.md) because with dense cross-document relationships the supervisor skips buckets whose domain looks irrelevant at first glance, so documents are distributed in no particular order and only balanced by count so the least amount of documents are needed. KV cache is expensive, but cost is reduced by optimizing how long each cache lives; there is no one-solution-fits-all, and every retrieval strategy trades compute, cost, and speed.
- [Design AI systems requirements-first with a four-phase framework](concepts/design-ai-systems-requirements-first-with-a-four-phase-framework.md) - Apoorva Joshi (Staff AI Developer Advocate, MongoDB; ex-cybersecurity data scientist) argues that when AI writes the code, the valuable skill is deciding *what* to build — "specs are the new code" — and most AI systems fail in production from bad decisions made *before* any code, so she gives a repeatable **four-phase** design framework walked end-to-end on a health-insurance claims-review assistant for medical reviewers at fictional "MDB Health." **(1) Product requirements:** quantify a *solution-agnostic* business problem (user-specific, current state, quantified pain with baselines, and no prescribed agent/multi-agent shape — "reviewers spend 2 days/claim, 4× the non-urgent and 12× the urgent industry standard"); gather **business constraints** (data-residency, approved-cloud models only, human-mandatory *denial* and complex cases) and **performance constraints** (latency, monthly LLM spend, uptime SLA) up front as design inputs; classify the **role of AI** along three dimensions (critical vs complementary; reactive vs proactive; level of autonomy → semi-autonomous when humans are mandatory); and state a **SMART** success metric (urgent claims 2 days → 1 hour within 90 days). **(2) System design:** a *per-source* data strategy (residence, update cadence so pipelines don't reason over stale data, processing, and retrieval technique — vector/hybrid + metadata pre-filter for terminology-heavy PDFs, exact match on patient ID); *map the request flow* first; then compose the **simplest** set of AI design patterns from a catalog (RAG, agents/multi-agent, control flow, LLM-as-router, human-in-the-loop, fine-tuning) — here RAG + control flow + human-in-the-loop — instead of jumping to an agent, because the common failure is over-engineering before knowing what's failing; then UX/feedback (citations for self-explanation, override + flag-bad-citation feedback) and a constraint-bounded stack. **(3) Evaluation and monitoring:** define input/output guardrails (LLM systems are probabilistic) and [layer application metrics from guardrail compliance to system health](concepts/layer-ai-application-metrics-from-guardrail-compliance-to-system-health.md) — guardrail-compliance rates (rejection rate, missing-citation rate), response quality (faithfulness), one domain metric (processing time), system health (token cost/usage, turns, cost per recommendation) — that carry into online **monitoring** plus implicit indicators (human-override rate, human review time). **(4) Optimize** accuracy (context-window control: prompt engineering, reranking, memory), cost/latency (semantic caching, batch processing), and reliability (structured outputs, API-failure handling) before ship. Core discipline: start simplest, evaluate, find gaps, iterate; "you can't improve what you can't measure," so build evaluation in from the start.
- [Bridge ML research to production with a taxonomy handoff document](concepts/bridge-ml-research-to-production-with-a-taxonomy-handoff-document.md) - Vaidas Razgaitis (senior research engineer, Higharc labs — home building + spatial reasoning, using CV to parse hand-sketched floor plans, reasoning agents, custom transformers, and diffusion models) treats the frontier-ML-research-to-production gap as a **systems-and-process** problem, not a modeling one: the friction is a two-sided handoff between platform/infra/back-end engineers (robust production code, but unfamiliar with CV/LLM-training methodology) and ML researchers (paper-fluent, but never owned production-grade APIs). Three velocity levers. (1) **Research legibility** via a *research prototype taxonomy* (RPT) document — a software-engineering technical design doc / RFC (the specs-before-building discipline) adapted for ML: ML-specific sections up front (domain context and novel data representations — a party diagram, a home's circulation graph, embedding/latent-space reps, framed as "what would a software engineer we just hired from JP Morgan need to know"; then the business goal/value) followed by conventional TDD sections (the type contract between the core product repo and the ML repo; the persistence layer — deliberately *not* where the researcher should sink time; the system architecture/anatomy; the merge/decompose plan), kept in Notion. (2) Code structure — [structure the ML codebase as decoupled per-researcher microservices](concepts/structure-the-ml-codebase-as-decoupled-per-researcher-microservices.md): a separate all-Python mono repo of cleanly isolated, fully decoupled microservices at roughly a one-researcher-to-one-microservice ratio, fronted by a **gateway** on one **Docker bridge network**, each a standalone **layered FastAPI** app (services → controllers → API routers; own Dockerfile + poetry/UV lock files), with GitHub Actions CI, Jupyter notebooks on **Modal** for GPU compute, a tooling CLI, and agent-navigable specs underneath. (3) **Decomposition** — slice the proven monolithic prototype along its dependency graph and land it as **Graphite stacked diffs** for asynchronous subject-matter-expert review, with the RPT's mapped layers/types informing the slicing. Closing per-lever diagnostics: ambiguity about where new staff should concentrate → legibility gap; fighting old abstractions → repo-structure gap; can't estimate timelines or name reviewers → decomposition/upstream-handoff gap.
- [Rank agent memory by outcome utility, not just similarity](concepts/rank-agent-memory-by-outcome-utility-not-just-similarity.md) - Sonam Pankaj (CEO, StarlightSearch; co-creator of the `embedanything` Rust RAG pipeline) argues production agents fail because the outcome signal from evals and observability never crosses back into what the agent does next — a ReAct agent reasons/calls tools/retrieves memory but has no loop to learn from what worked, so it starts every run from a blank slate and repeats the same failure (she cites Gartner "85% of AI failing traction," McKinsey 2025, "73% of pipelines fail at retrieval not generation," and a Ram Sriram / ex-Pinecone-CTO line, "we forgot to make retrieval learn"). The retrieval-side fix is a **utility score**: retrieve memories not by embedding similarity alone but by similarity *weighted by whether each memory historically helped or hurt the run's outcome* — memories behave like a **credit score** (retrieved + run passed → utility rises; run failed → utility falls), so outcome is a first-class re-ranking signal, and memory is treated as **reasoning** ("if someone asks for a refund, check the settlement first so they aren't paid twice") not static preferences, which also combats context stuffing. The architecture-side fix is to [close the eval-to-action loop so signal survives the dashboard](concepts/close-the-eval-to-action-loop-so-signal-survives-the-dashboard.md): observability captures every tool call and the eval suite judges pass/fail, but that verdict is never reflected in the agent's context/skills/actions — "the eval signal dies in the dashboard" — so a runtime "Agent RX" layer (their product "Reflect") consumes traces, absorbs eval outcomes, and converts both into retrieval guidance so the agent improves *while executing*, with no retrain, fine-tune, or manual prompt rewrite (contrasted with compile-time DSPy-style baking), and once ~10 outcome-labeled memories accumulate it bakes the stabilized reasoning into a **skill** the agent always calls (fixing e.g. a stale column still named in a SQL agent's system prompt that no system updates today). Reported lift on tau-bench (policy adherence): 66%→76%, 80% with skills; limitations are cold start (pure semantic search until enough reviews), utility drift, noisy review labels, and a credit/re-ranking hyperparameter `lambda`.
- [Fix the browser-agent runtime interface before reaching for a better model](concepts/fix-the-browser-agent-runtime-interface-before-reaching-for-a-better-model.md) - Kushan Raj (ARK) argues browser agents underperform because the **runtime interface** around the model is poor, not because the model is weak: every recent improvement has been a model upgrade (better vision, longer context, smarter planning) yet agents still fail on basic workflows. His fix rebuilds three levers — what the model **sees** (a compact whole-page markdown representation instead of a raw DOM dump or a single screenshot: for one page the full DOM ≈ 20,000 tokens, a screenshot ≈ 1,100 tokens but shows only the visible viewport, while [the markdown ≈ 1,800 tokens captures the *entire* page](concepts/give-browser-agents-a-compact-whole-page-representation.md) and pairs with a screenshot for cheap visual grounding), what it can **do** (fast actions with stable handles instead of one click per model call, so it plans long sequences), and what it **learns from** (step-by-step delta feedback from tracking the full end-to-end page — "these popped up," "this is gone," "you tried to click this but it didn't happen" — instead of a final pass/fail). Thesis: "models are pretty smart, but it's the infra around them that sucks"; changing the interface alone took the *same, cheaper* model from confusion to correct multi-step execution on hostile pages where default computer-use (Claude on an Aadhaar download, a Canadian booking site) stalled. The agent-side counterpart to the site-side WebMCP surfaces, and the browser instance of "choose the observation/action space deliberately."
- [Match the agent's output medium to its native representation](concepts/match-agent-output-medium-to-its-native-representation.md) - Amol Kapoor (CEO, Nori) reframes the belief that coding agents "can't do graphics" (the ARC-AGI / Simon Willison SVG "pelican riding a bicycle" skepticism) as a **medium** problem, not a model limit: human canvas tools (PowerPoint, Figma, Canva, and their agent wrappers — Figma MCPs, PowerPoint CLIs, screenshot-and-replace loops) force the model to place coordinates in "a wall of numbers," as foreign to a model as handwriting SVG is to a person. Give the model tools that match how it thinks — "not in pixels, in language: words, tokens, structure" — and it becomes competent: **HTML** lets it reason in semantic structure (heading, chart, grid) while the browser renders to pixels, "so the model never actually places a coordinate," yielding charts/layouts/fonts/motion "for free" and staying readable/themeable/editable. The operational form is to [author visual artifacts as HTML and decouple the editing format from delivery](concepts/author-visual-artifacts-as-html-decoupled-from-delivery-format.md): HTML/CSS is the universal editing substrate for slides, docs, and video (Nori builds all its decks, its docs, and the talk's own video this way — "divs all the way down"), while the delivery format (a PowerPoint deck, a PDF) is "just the presentation mode" rendered later — the artifact-generation instance of "prefer a text-native surface over spatial automation." Takeaway: "Stop thinking like a user. Think like the model."
- [Correlate entities across documents to surface cross-document risk](concepts/correlate-entities-across-documents-to-surface-cross-document-risk.md) - Varsha Shah (enterprise architect, TCS/Microsoft) presents a research framework for enterprise financial compliance and fraud detection built on the thesis that the most consequential fraud "exists between documents, not within them": a payroll record, vendor invoice, and tax filing can each pass independent validation while their *relationships* reveal the anomaly, so rule-based and document-level NLP systems — designed to validate individual records — structurally cannot catch cross-system patterns. The fix is a **graph-based entity correlation engine** that links employees, vendors, accounts, transactions, and regulatory filings across payroll/tax/procurement/financial systems into one network to expose structural anomalies ("what is connected?"). Riding on that substrate, the framework [learns risk scoring from audit feedback for predictive compliance](concepts/learn-risk-scoring-from-audit-feedback-for-predictive-compliance.md): an **adaptive probabilistic risk model** fuses anomaly strength, source reliability, and historic patterns into a confidence-weighted score that prioritizes cases (not per-rule alerts), and a **feedback loop** — confirmed frauds strengthen detection patterns, false positives refine scoring — plus a **cross-jurisdictional normalization layer** (currencies, tax rules, reporting periods) shifts compliance from reactive validation to predictive governance ("what is likely to go wrong next" vs "what went wrong"), adapting as fraud evolves without manual rule rewrites. Evaluated on ~3M records over 5 years across 4 jurisdictions: ~91% precision, 87% recall, F1 0.89, 76% fewer false positives, ~40% less manual audit effort. (Research/architecture talk; no code-level detail.)
- [Spec-driven development is a tool-portable pattern, not a single product](concepts/spec-driven-development-is-a-tool-portable-pattern.md) - Erik Hanchett (AWS) makes the introductory case that structured requirements → design → task-list Markdown written *before* code guides coding assistants — which behave like over-eager "AI interns" that "go off the rails" without it and steer better on structured context than a bare prompt — and, most durably, that the discipline is a **pattern, not a product**: the same requirements→design→tasks flow runs manually with any assistant (ask for user requirements, review, then design, review, then the implementation task list), via GitHub's open-source **Spec Kit** ("Spec It"), or via **Kiro** (AWS's AI IDE spec mode / CLI plan mode, GA'd late 2025 out of a bespoke pattern customers were already hand-rolling), so the reusable value is the reviewable artifact chain plus a human review gate, not vendor lock-in. A concrete operational lever rides on top — [reorder the generated task list to ship an MVP first](concepts/reorder-the-generated-task-list-to-ship-an-mvp-first.md): once the task list exists, tell the agent to pull the top four tasks to the front as a self-contained MVP and build that first so a running slice appears before the full spec is implemented (in the demo Kiro reframed requirements so tasks 1-4 delivered a working browsable movie grid with search/filter/sort/theme). The talk also reinforces the topic's standing threads — the human as accountable code reviewer of all generated code and docs, Goldilocks-zone AGENTS.md/CLAUDE.md steering plus on-demand skills, EARS requirements with clarifying questions, mermaid/ASCII design docs you must edit with your own taste, property-based tests (fast-check) generated against the requirements/design docs, greenfield-and-legacy applicability with a bug-fix spec mode, and MCP pulling Jira/Asana tickets into spec generation.
- [Structure Documents Offline to Avoid the Multimodal Token Tax](concepts/structure-documents-offline-to-avoid-the-multimodal-token-tax.md) - Abed Matini (Ogilvy) live-demos a framework-free, fully local RAG chatbot (HR FAQ assistant on Python/FastAPI, React, Postgres/pgvector, Docker, Ollama, Docling, Langfuse; no GPU, one command on Codespaces) whose headline is the **"multimodal tax"**: drag-and-dropping a raw PDF/Word/image into a cloud chatbot both spends tokens on document processing *before any question is asked* and *hides* how the file was chunked and read (a badly-read table you can't see → lower accuracy, more hallucination), so the fix is structure-first offline ingestion — Docling → Markdown on local CPU → a known chunking strategy → pgvector — making ingestion a one-time, cheaper-or-free, traceable step where every answer cites its source chunk (1-2 pages is negligible but a 200-page handbook × many employees re-asking makes naive upload expensive). Because no single chunker fits every document, the build [matches a chunking strategy to document type](concepts/match-chunking-strategy-to-document-type.md): heading-based for FAQ Q&A (each heading+answer a clean traceable chunk), paragraph-based for prose, fixed 512-char with 64-char overlap for unorganized data, sentence-based for short messages, and image→OCR→sentence for ad-hoc screenshots — since dumping a whole handbook leaves meaningless fragments (acknowledgement, signature, date) that raise hallucination. Retrieval is hybrid pgvector nearest-neighbor + BM25 exact-match (SKU/brand/medication/ID) fused with **RRF in SQL** and reranked, with [top-K tuned per use case](concepts/hybrid-retrieval-combines-lexical-sparse-dense-and-reranking-signals.md) (products retrieve more or same-brand items never surface; medical fewer for accuracy/liability; default top-2). The same talk reinforces wider threads: "agents" are plain Python functions not LLM loops (speed on local Ollama, full control, testable), guardrails are enforced in code before the LLM (tiny system prompt; medical-escalation and prompt-injection blocked via intent rejects, term dictionaries, and an LLM classifier), Langfuse gives local per-conversation telemetry (model, top-K, chunk count, latency ms, cost estimate), and once data is vetted the smallest model (Qwen 2.5 0.5B ~400MB + BGE embed, no GPU) suffices, hallucinates less, and safely abstains ("I don't have that information") rather than fabricating.
- [Relax the Latency Budget by Choosing Voice-In, Visuals-Out Over Voice-Out](concepts/relax-the-latency-budget-with-voice-in-visuals-out.md) - Allen Pike (Forestwalk Labs), following Karpathy, argues the pinnacle of AI UX is voice-in, visuals-out: voice is the highest-bandwidth human *input* (more words/min than typing and more meaning per word — "okay" vs "okay") while rich visuals, now generatable as HTML/interactive controls/illustrations via tool calling, are the preferred *output*. The decisive engineering payoff is latency — since the '60s ~100 ms feels instant, ~1 s is the train-of-thought limit, and full voice-in/voice-out demands ~200 ms to feel conversive, a "ridiculous" amount of work (network + STT + inference + network) to fit in 200 ms. Rather than wait for novel 200 ms-slice architectures (Thinking Machines/Neolab's time-sliced continuous inference), voice-in/visuals-out escapes the trap: something on screen within ~1 s still feels seamless, a ~5× more forgiving envelope (Forestwalk's in-call voice agent filed a Linear issue on spoken intent within a second). Inside that ~1 s envelope, [three levers hit soft-realtime latency](concepts/hit-realtime-latency-with-fast-models-eager-inference-and-prefix-caching.md): a latency-prioritized fast model (small isn't enough — GPT-5 mini showed 5-10 s P95 where Haiku stays responsive) that hands heavy work off asynchronously to a larger thinking model and re-interleaves it; eager short-interval inference fired every 1-2 s as the user talks instead of waiting for end-of-speech silence; and a stable prefix-caching regimen holding ~90% of the context fixed request-to-request while minimizing output tokens for up to 90% cheaper/faster turns.
- [Choose a Document-Extraction Tier by Cost, Determinism, and Scale](concepts/choose-document-extraction-tier-by-cost-determinism-and-scale.md) - Cedric Clyburn (Red Hat) frames unstructured-document ingestion — the "new context layer for AI" — as a tiered decision, not a single tool pick, live-demoing the open-source **Docling** library. A naive PDF parser is fast/cheap on CPU but linearizes and truncates tables, leaks headers, and drops images; a frontier VLM is high quality but ~$30/M output tokens, non-deterministic (hallucination risk at scale), and brittle to model deprecations (a 5.1→5.2 change breaks consistent structured output); a local structure-aware library like Docling is the deterministic, air-gappable, GPU-optional middle ground, and ~50× cheaper at scale (Hugging Face's FinePDFs corpus was built on CPU with Docling + OCR). Extraction accuracy is not cosmetic: a column-merge OCR error produced a nonexistent term now cited across ~20 scientific papers. Once a document is parsed into faithful structure, retrieval can skip the vector store entirely — [chunkless/agentic RAG](concepts/use-a-document-outline-as-the-retrieval-index-for-chunkless-agentic-rag.md) treats the document's Markdown section outline as the whole retrieval index and an agent iterates over section summaries, pulling the relevant section's full text (no chunker, embedding model, or vector database; demoed over a 418-section annual report). The production surface strengthens the [structure-aware parsing](concepts/structure-aware-document-parsing-improves-rag-chunk-quality.md) story: a Pydantic `DoclingDocument` enables table→DataFrame and structured single-field extraction, the layout model can strip PII before extraction, VLMs (local Granite via Ollama) enrich images into retrievable descriptions, `docling serve` runs a REST microservice for thousands of docs, and a Docling MCP server exposes conversion/generation/manipulation tools to coding agents.
- [Compose Domain-Specific Agents Instead of Inflating One Agent's Context](concepts/compose-domain-specific-agents-instead-of-inflating-one-context.md) - Justin Schroeder (StandardAgents) names the architectural choice under multi-agent design as **composition over inheritance**. The default "integration" pattern stacks skills + MCP servers onto one large general-purpose agent whose runtime is "almost all context" (model → system prompt → tools → skills → MCP → messages) — *inheritance* that works but breaks down (5 skills fine, 100/1000 hits diminishing returns; "using very many [skills] makes your agent substantially worse"), while robust single agents are also hard to build, not portable ("works on my machine"), and not composable. The alternative is many tiny full domain-specific agents, each with a system prompt that *is* the domain expertise (a Figma agent that knows Figma's APIs/clicks/mouse-moves), only its precise tools, and its own small message history + agentic loop, coordinated by a top-level agent that delegates in plain English (the Apollo mission-control shape; "we didn't land a man on the moon by giving one guy a ton of tools," and MCP has become mainly a *tool*-distribution mechanism while skills are just documentation). Specialists nest recursively (coordinator → Salesforce → Workspace/asset-gen → legal → GDPR → OSHA) so a large domain context (~45MB of GDPR) lives in its own sub-agent instead of bloating the parent. This [unlocks small models and tight permissions](concepts/domain-specific-agents-unlock-small-models-and-tight-permissions.md): 80%+ token efficiency per task (a specialist's total context is just its prompt, its tools, and one incoming instruction — "get that last email from Debbie"), cheap models viable on narrow tasks (DeepSeek V4 Flash ~137× cheaper per task than Fable 5; non-language image/diffusion models can be agents too), per-agent capability limits instead of big-agent permission-bypassing, and clean parallel/cloud scaling plus portability ("squeeze up" a Gmail agent and hand it off). The economic driver is a claimed 2026 reversal in falling token cost (per-token up ~29–30% adjusted for IQ, ~76% unadjusted mid-year), and the "ideal agent" anatomy adds a tool layer split into functions / injectable prompts (a tool that calls an LLM, e.g. Nano Banana while GLM 5.2 is primary) / a full sub-agent-as-tool, plus hooks (inject a synthetic "what time is it?" tool call), agent rules (max turns/steps, tool-call validation), and baked-in sandbox filesystem + code execution. Predictions: rest of 2026 = a rapid uptick in domain-specific-agent frameworks (Vercel's Eve cited as first to publicly use the term); 2027 = "the year of multi-agent orchestration."
- [Automate the Agent-Building Loop With an Agentic AI Engineer](concepts/automate-the-agent-building-loop-with-an-agentic-ai-engineer.md) - Benedikt Sanftl (CEO) and Burak (CTO) of Mutagent turn the coding-agent loop on the task of *building agents themselves*: an "Agentic AI Engineer" is a multi-agent team steered by an orchestrator across spec, build, evaluate, ship, monitor, diagnose, and optimize, run as two connected loops — an offline loop (spec → build → evaluate → ship) and an online loop (monitor → diagnose → optimize → feed back). Done by hand the loop is slow because "the bottleneck basically becomes the human review and the human building time," which can't scale once an org runs tens or hundreds of agents; running each stage agentically fits many more cycles into the same window. Building splits into a cold-start path (spec-driven design from scratch, kept implementation-independent so the harness stays a portable choice as frameworks like Hermes and deep agents churn) and an existing-feature path (optimize an agent that already runs). Eval-driven development (EDD) is the TDD-equivalent that supplies the agent's termination gate ("when is an agent good enough?"): the eval suite is evals (metrics/criteria) + datasets (cases), and its complete form is "a product of discovery" you can't pre-guess — seed it with domain experts or historical/synthesized data and let production failures and user feedback accumulate edge cases. Agent evals run over the trajectory (context completeness + chain-checking every tool output), useful evals give actionable feedback (prefer binary/criteria over score-based unless the rubric is very well defined), and the LLM-as-judge must be calibrated to control run-to-run variance. The diagnosis stage — [code-checkable indicators and sampling](concepts/diagnose-agent-failures-with-code-checkable-indicators-and-sampling.md) — clusters production failures by root cause via a recursive why-chain and distills a code-checkable indicator per failure mode (a content pattern or tool-call sequence) so you triage a representative sample instead of deep-reading millions of traces (reading them all "costs more than the execution itself"), then generates detect-evals and remedies. Productized as Mutagent: an evaluator agent and a diagnostics agent in research preview, connected by an orchestrator in your coding environment, with connectors to Langfuse / local Claude transcripts / exported JSONL and targets like an auto-raised GitHub PR or edits to agent `.md` files.
- [Right-Size Models With Prototype Big, Deploy Small](concepts/right-size-models-with-prototype-big-deploy-small.md) - RL Nabors (Arize) argues most frontier-model calls are wasteful and should be replaced by small/local models you measure your way to, not guess at. The cost of one-size-fits-all cloud inference is security (data leaves the stack), latency (~4s believability limit, many frontier calls exceed it), uncontrollable and compounding business cost, offline failure, and energy (an SLM burns ~25% of an LLM's per-task energy, a task-specific model half of that; Nvidia's 2025 paper calls SLMs "the future of agentic AI"). Her framework — "prototype big, deploy small" — makes model choice an eval: prove the feature is possible on the largest model, freeze its behavior as a golden dataset of success criteria, run a capability eval benchmarking candidate small models in a mixed scoring harness (JSON validity via `JSON.parse`, reference structural validity, LLM-as-judge factual consistency, length, P50/P95 latency) in Arize's open-source Phoenix, then select the SAGE ("small and good enough") model. In her Mima summarization case a Llama 3.2 3B (~90% accuracy, fast) beat both a slower Gemma 4 5B and the model peers recommended, and she [closed the residual gap](concepts/close-the-small-model-gap-with-prompt-variants-and-harness-post-processing.md) by testing prompt variants one variable at a time (few-shot beat strict-rules, which the small model "responded very negatively to," and chain-of-thought, which added latency) plus deterministic harness post-processing (reference-count checks, length truncation) — reaching 100% structural validity and beating the baseline on latency without a bigger model. A hazard she surfaces: [LLM judges show self-preference and family bias](concepts/llm-judges-show-self-preference-and-family-bias.md) — Claude judging Claude-vs-Llama graded the outsider too strictly, so the factual-consistency gap was judge bias caught only by reading the raw responses. Lock gains in with regression evals run like CI/CD; on-device APIs already ship (Chrome prompt API → Gemini Nano, Pixel 10 Pro).
- [Choose a Skill's Trigger by Trading Context Load Against Cognitive Load](concepts/choose-skill-trigger-by-trading-context-load-against-cognitive-load.md) - Matt Pocock (author of the `mattpocock/skills` repo) gives a shared rubric — a four-axis "skill checklist" — for telling good agent skills from bad and escaping "skill hell" (lots of downloadable skills, no way to tell which are effective). **Trigger**: a skill is always user-invocable (file on disk, human points the agent at it via `/`), and becomes model-invocable when its description sits in context as a "context pointer" the agent may follow (`disable model invocation: true` makes it user-only); the choice is a symmetric-cost tradeoff, not a default — model-invoked adds a description costing tokens on every request plus firing unpredictability that forces skill evals (context load), while user-invoked stays deterministic but the pilot must remember and deeply understand the skills (cognitive load); superpowers is mostly model-invoked, his own repo mostly user-invoked. **Structure**: compose a skill from steps + reference, keep `SKILL.md` as small as possible, and hide branch-specific reference behind context pointers to external files. **Steering**: [leading words](concepts/steer-agents-with-leading-words-that-surface-in-reasoning-traces.md) — a dense phrase like *vertical slice* dropped into the skill that the agent echoes into its reasoning traces (verify the steer by reading the traces; "English is a wide API") — and [splitting a skill to hide future steps](concepts/split-skills-to-hide-future-steps-and-force-leg-work.md) so the agent does more leg work on the current phase instead of rushing a visible goal (his `grill with docs` clarifying skill runs before `to PRD`, fixing plan mode rushing its questions). **Pruning**: keep skills small via [a single source of truth, sediment removal, and no-op deletion tests](concepts/prune-skills-with-single-source-of-truth-sediment-and-no-op-deletion-tests.md) — massive skills are a symptom, and a "deletion test" finds instructions that don't change behavior. Packaged as a `writing great skills` skill in his repo.
- [Record and Replay Agent Runs at Node Boundaries](concepts/record-and-replay-agent-runs-at-node-boundaries.md) - Tisha Chawla and Susheem Koul (Microsoft) argue the real failure mode of production agents is not the bad decision but the inability to reproduce it: a non-deterministic run that corrupts a record (a broker agent told to "sell $1,000" sold 1,000 shares, ~$190k, while the API returned a clean 200 OK and dashboards stayed green) rarely repeats its trajectory, so re-running the prompt succeeds every time and the one costly run is gone — "if you can't reproduce it, you can't debug it; if you can't debug it, you can't promise it won't happen to your next customer." They debunk the temperature-zero reflex because [LLM inference is non-deterministic even at temperature zero](concepts/llm-inference-is-non-deterministic-even-at-temperature-zero.md): argmax fixes token selection but not the underlying scores, floating-point math isn't associative, and the true culprits are server-side batch invariance (a request is grouped with whatever hits the server that millisecond) and MoE routing (an overflowing batch reroutes tokens by co-batched traffic). The reframe: stop chasing bitwise determinism (controllability you can't get from a hosted API and don't want — randomness "brings the agency into your agent") and build replayability (observability) — don't freeze the model, record what it did. Record at the *boundary*, not the network layer (half the agent never touches the network), wrapping each node (tool call, LLM call, RAG retrieval) so its semantic input/output and session variables (model version, build ID, RAG chunks) freeze into a trace you can re-enter with zero model calls. Because you can't control the LLM, guardrail the deterministic tools and verify by replay — which lets a recorded run [become a free regression test case](concepts/turn-recorded-agent-traces-into-free-replay-test-cases.md): stub every node except the one under change, run it live, assert the order got blocked; deterministic testing stubs the LLM (free, model-free) while behavioral testing (tone, trajectory) uses LLM-as-judge. This is Mozilla-rr record-and-replay in the agent loop, and durability (keeping the loop alive) is explicitly not debuggability (re-entering the historical run).
- [Treat the Specification as the Product and Derive Bespoke Implementations](concepts/treat-the-specification-as-the-product-and-derive-bespoke-implementations.md) - Dominik Tornow (Resonate, durable execution) argues coding agents will "quietly retire their first software platform" — not because it's bad but because it's *unnecessary*: general-purpose implementations get replaced by bespoke ones generated on demand as minimal extensions of infrastructure already in place, so reuse and value move upstream from implementation to an abstract, target-agnostic *specification* ("the prompt is a platform, the specification is a product"), and the real question is whether you can *repeatedly synthesize trusted* servers from one spec. The engineering lesson: prompting an agent straight from an abstract spec to a Rust-on-Postgres server produced happy-path code that broke on concurrency, process, and network failure, so Resonate inserted a deterministic simulation step and has the agent [build a simulated implementation as executable design](concepts/use-deterministic-simulation-as-executable-design-for-agents.md) (abstract spec → simulation → concrete spec → concrete implementation) — moving it upstream into *design*, not just build-and-verify. The debugging mechanism generalizes: because agents thrive on feedback that shows *why* a run failed, the simulator [exposes "forbidden-fruit" facts](concepts/expose-forbidden-fruit-facts-in-simulation-for-agent-debugging.md) the real platform hides (whether a KV read was fresh or stale, and the latest value the algorithm missed) that the *algorithm* may not depend on but the *agent* may use to explain why an invariant broke — the invariant failed *because* the algorithm decided from a stale view of the world. Enablers: minimalism as the finish line (three years shrinking the protocol to a durable promise and a durable task) and expressing the protocol using only the target's primitives while staying correct under its real consistency model, not convenient behavior.
- [Build an Agentic Control Plane So the Model Proposes and the Platform Decides](concepts/build-an-agentic-control-plane.md) - Nishant Gupta (Meta Superintelligence Labs) argues that as organizations move from chatbots to autonomous agents, "the challenge is no longer intelligence — the challenge is reliability," and most platforms were built for deterministic microservices, not long-running non-deterministic LLM systems ("the great mismatch": agents are stateful, long-running, decide dynamically, and may run different workflows for the same input). Demos showcase capability ("can it do it?"); production asks whether it can do it reliably "10,000 times, 100,000 times, a million times," recover from failures, and stay within a cost/latency/outcome budget — so engineering effort "moves below the model layer into orchestration, monitoring, safety evaluation, and recovery." His central move: "never let the model directly control production systems… the model generates proposals, infrastructure validates, policy engine approves, execution gateway enforces; the model just suggests, the platform decides," so reliability holds even when the model stays stochastic — an emerging **agentic control plane** that is the Kubernetes/service-mesh analog ("an operating system for autonomous AI"). The failures it contains are infrastructural, not hallucinations: [retry amplification](concepts/contain-retry-amplification-in-agent-loops.md) (an agent regenerating slightly-different-but-still-invalid requests until "a minor API error became a compute incident" — "one of the biggest risks in agentic systems"), recursive loops, deadlocks, context corruption, and memory poisoning, because "the model makes a mistake, but the infrastructure turns that mistake into an outage." His durable claim is that most of this is not new — [treat agents as distributed systems](concepts/treat-multi-agent-systems-as-distributed-systems.md) and reuse the primitives (circuit breakers → tool isolation, rate limits → agent limits, retries → controlled recovery, quotas → cost governance, observability → agent tracing), remembering that "many multi-agent failures are actually consistency failures masquerading as reasoning failures" — with layered defense-in-depth safety, humans as permanent exception handlers, and GPU inference as a cluster-scheduling problem, closing that as prompts and models commoditize, "the future of AI won't be won by better prompts — it will be won by better systems."
- [Elicit Requirements as the Non-Automatable Bottleneck](concepts/elicit-requirements-as-the-non-automatable-bottleneck.md) - Balázs Horváth (VisualLabs) argues that once AI makes code, specs, and tests cheap, the software-delivery bottleneck moves upstream to people work — getting the right stakeholders in the room and naming what's worth building — because "you can prompt your AI… but you can't prompt the room." His evidence is a VisualLabs hackathon that abandoned 17 of 21 agent ideas for lack of business value (no data access, no owner, no measurable value); the 4 that qualified run in production. Since a model "is coded to give you the most common answers," naive use replicates a Ford "faster horse," so a human must read the room and name the problem to reach the car. His remedy resurfaces the business-analyst toolkit as newly scarce and AI-native: [capture what to build with value discovery, story maps, and trained user-story structures](concepts/capture-ai-build-requirements-with-story-maps-and-user-stories.md) — run the VAD path (Value → Architecture → Design) and a 4-question value framework (whose problem, what winning looks like, what causes refusal, what decision it changes) tracked in a repo markdown file for AI context, then story-map the process backbone and write persona/what/need/why user stories with acceptance criteria (which become test cases), because models were trained on that structure. The delivery consequence is a metric shift: [measure feature adoption, not shipping velocity](concepts/measure-feature-adoption-not-shipping-velocity.md) — replace "features shipped" with "features used more than twice," watch activity frequency instead of time-on-site, reject the demo-as-deliverable and testerless-PRD anti-patterns, and move the smartest people from writing code to naming customer problems, "because that's the expensive part."
- [Treat AI as an Interface Technology That Removes Human Burden](concepts/treat-ai-as-an-interface-technology-that-removes-human-burden.md) - Ted Johnson (JoinIn AI) reframes AI as an *interface* technology, not only an intelligence one, to explain why capable models still "feel unnatural to use." He splits any interface into channel (physical transport/bandwidth — keyboard, mic, prompt box), expression (the meaning it's permitted to carry), and protocol (the rules of the exchange): LLMs *exploded* expression (assembly opcodes → shell flags → programming primitives → natural language, which "blew the menu open") while the channel stayed a box and [the prompt kept the punch-card batch protocol](concepts/treat-the-prompt-as-a-batch-protocol-not-a-conversation.md) — package a complete turn, submit, wait, resubmit — so "prompt engineering" is just the operator's skill of assembling a good deck and "shorter batch is still batch." The mismatch (model capacity curving up, protocol flat) leaves the human carrying every interface burden and blaming themselves when it fails ("the mismatch isn't the user, it's the interface"). The fix is to ask "what burden are we still putting on humans only because the machine used to be too limited to carry it?" and let the AI *participate* mid-thought with the affordances humans already use (a question, a pause, a checklist, a quiet aside, silence) — but genuine participation needs multi-party awareness (who's speaking, whether the words were meant for it), which backchanneling and even learned turn-taking do not by themselves solve; the reflex answers (everything is chat, or voice, or a wall of markdown) are usually wrong.
- [Use MCP App Primitives for Bidirectional Model–UI State](concepts/use-mcp-app-primitives-for-bidirectional-model-ui-state.md) - Pietro Zullo (Manufact, maintainers of the `mcp-use` SDK) argues an MCP App is "not just an MCP server with a UI bolted on" but a full bidirectional interaction layer: the tool returns a `ui://` widget rendered by the host in a sandboxed iframe, and beyond that first render a set of primitives keeps the model and the UI in sync — the widget can push its live state into the model's context *without* a user message (set-state / update-model-context), send a follow-up message into the conversation unprompted (Claude prefills the chat input, OpenAI auto-sends and streams), call other tools, and receive the model's tool-call arguments streamed in *incrementally* so the UI draws as tokens arrive (Excalidraw mermaid-to-canvas, a Remotion React video rendering live). A privacy primitive matters for tool design: a tool returns a *list* of outputs, so you can send rich structured data into the widget while returning a separate redacted output (or nothing but "the user is seeing their private information above") to the model, enabling agent UX where sharing raw data with the model provider is disallowed. The other half of his thesis is distribution: the client stores (ChatGPT apps, Claude connectors, Cursor directory) opened for self-serve submission and one-click install URLs, and Claude already does [dynamic discovery — searching the MCP registry for the right connector](concepts/distribute-mcp-apps-through-stores-and-dynamic-discovery.md) when a task has no matching tool, making store presence a high-intent organic acquisition channel ("AI apps are the new browsers, ChatGPTs are the new websites").
- [Operate Agent Products as the Missing Post-Launch Layer](concepts/operate-agent-products-as-the-missing-post-launch-layer.md) - Raphael Kalandadze (Wandero AI, an agent-native OS for travel/hospitality) argues shipping an agent is now the *easy* part — "the shipping is the moment when the real work begins" — and the neglected "missing layer" is operating it after launch, a discipline unlike classical software because a non-deterministic agent's failure is *silent*: "nothing crashes and no log lights up; the failure is in the conversation, not the stack trace." An agent can struggle mid-task, get lucky, recover with a workaround, and finish green while having failed the customer (built the itinerary but used the wrong service and mispriced it — "technically successful but still failing the task"), so "green CI means nothing for a non-deterministic agent" and unit tests cover only "one slice" — "production is the place where you learn what you need to test in the first place." Because separating a real bug from noise and a symptom from a root cause needs reasoning, "the operating agent itself is an agent problem," so his answer is to [staff operations with a team of agents](concepts/staff-agent-operations-with-a-team-of-agents.md) — a "meta harness" of a fast log-monitor→PR agent (rolling window over logs/trajectories with code-base access, drafting PRs with mermaid/ASCII/HTML artifacts, PR-ready in ~30 min), a *fresh-context* review agent that criticizes each PR (root cause or just the symptom?) because agents are "eager to send the PR" and biased toward their own fix, a session analyzer that [scores every production conversation for system health](concepts/score-every-production-conversation-to-judge-agent-health.md) (not a sample, into a health dashboard that connects the dots into patterns), and a computer-use agent that logs in and simulates the customer to catch UI failures logs can't see — with humans kept only at merge boundaries (the PR + review agents "send 10 times more PRs than the three of us every day") under the staging rule "close the loop first, then you can remove yourself." The unifying principle: "it's not the model alone," so give every operating agent the full context a human would need (trajectories, metrics, database, UI) and build the harness that "watches itself, understands, improves," because everyone can copy the model and harness — the differentiator is the internal operations system that closes the loop.
- [Explain an AI Product as a Wound → Click → Transformation Story](concepts/explain-ai-products-as-a-wound-click-transformation-story.md) - Veronica Hylak (Hey AI) argues that in a market where shipping is easy, "great tech that nobody understands dies quietly," and you get about an elevator ride to answer "why does this matter to an average user?" Her three-beat fix turns a complex AI product into a story people "instantly understand, remember, and want to buy": (1) open on **the wound** — the user's biggest pain, not what you built ("do not start with what you built"; in ~20 seconds identify the wound, say "we fix that," show how), because "we built an agentic orchestration SecOps platform for enterprises" makes people "feel nothing"; (2) [make it click with a concrete mental image, not a category label](concepts/give-a-concrete-mental-image-instead-of-a-category-label.md) — pass the 17-year-old test, tie to a viral story people already understand (the McDonald's AI drive-thru), ban unpicturable words, and give an image ("Devin, the AI software engineer"; "a smoke alarm for AI behavior") as a *front door* before the technical definition; (3) show **the transformation** with an explicit before/after ("30 minutes digging through docs → one question, answer in 10 seconds with sources"), because "if I cannot see what changed, I do not feel the story." This is the user-facing communication complement to distribution/GTM strategy: "the products with the clearest stories are the ones that get funded, bought, and talked about."
- [Verifiable Continual Learning: Prove Each Agent Fix Helps and Breaks Nothing](concepts/verifiable-continual-learning-prove-each-fix-helps-and-breaks-nothing.md) - Soheil Feizi (RELAI; CS professor, University of Maryland) frames continual learning for agents as learning from production experience — act, get feedback, improve *without forgetting* — and argues the discipline that makes it safe is **verifiable continual learning**: every fix "proven to help and proven to break nothing that already worked," via an executable test, a measured before/after delta, and a regression test. Two challenges structure the talk. Feedback: dev-time benchmarks give evaluators, but in production "production logs are not learning environments" — a log-plus-feedback pair is one observation, so you must [ground simulation in it and infer a distribution](concepts/ground-agent-simulation-and-evaluation-in-production-logs.md) (real vs mock tools, synthetic users, inferred evaluators) into a replayable, gradable environment. Acting: [route the fix to the right layer with the smallest durable change](concepts/route-agent-repairs-to-the-right-layer-smallest-durable-change.md) across model (SFT/RL/LoRA — expensive), harness (prompts/skills/tools/code — trace-to-harness is flexible but "vibe-based," GEPA prompt-search is testable but needs a benchmark), and memory (facts + skill distillation via Letta/Mem0 — cheapest but usually unverified), because "agent continual learning is not necessarily model fine-tuning." His four principles are replayability, holisticness, lifelongness, and efficiency, and the frontier he names is [regression-aware optimization](concepts/make-regression-aware-optimization-part-of-the-continual-learning-loop.md) — fix the new failure subject to *no regression* on past environments, in-loop rather than post-hoc, kept sub-linear in the number of accumulated environments. RELAI packages this as a loop (signals → learning environments → root-cause routing → regression-aware optimize → reviewable PR) added with a one-time harness setup plus two commands.
- [Capability Overhang: Tools Decide Which Model Spikes You Reach](concepts/capability-overhang-tools-decide-which-model-spikes-you-reach.md) - Thariq Shihipar (Anthropic, Claude Code) gives a "field guide to Fable" whose spine is that models get smarter in **spiky** ways and the tools decide which spikes you reach: a chat model can't name the Pokémon ending in "aw" though it knows them all, but Claude Code writes a script and filters in seconds. Because "the models are grown, not designed… what contains them is us," two concrete harness moves follow — [shrink the system prompt and drop examples as models improve](concepts/shrink-the-system-prompt-and-drop-examples-as-models-improve.md) (Claude Code cut 80% of its system prompt because "examples constrain a model more imaginative than the examples"; "give context, not constraints"; the ask-user-question tool ratcheted from barely-callable under Opus 4 to embedded-HTML questionnaires under Fable, so re-test old tools) and, on the human side, [use the agent to surface your own unknowns](concepts/use-the-agent-to-surface-your-own-unknowns.md) (the spec is a *map*, the codebase the *territory*, every gap an "unknown"; find them with a blind-spot pass, prototype variants, an interview, a reference codebase/mock-up as another map, implementation-note logging, and a comprehension quiz). His closing mindset — "tradeoffs are not real," demand good/fast/cheap at once and [rescope ambition up](concepts/rescope-ambition-down-a-tier-as-models-improve.md), with the caveat that "building is easier, but generating value is still hard."
